LARE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Stipulated sum, where the firm agrees to a fixed fee for a defined scope of services). A stipulated sum, or lump sum, contract sets a single fixed fee for a clearly defined scope, giving the client the cost certainty described in the scenario. Cost-plus and hourly-with-ceiling arrangements still expose the client to some cost variability tied to actual time and materials. Unit price contracts are typically used for construction, not design services, and price is set per installed quantity rather than as a total design fee.
  2. The correct answer is A (Qualifications-based selection, where technical merit is judged before fee negotiation begins). QBS separates the evaluation of a firm's qualifications and experience from fee negotiation, which only begins after the most qualified firm is identified, a common requirement on federally funded and many public projects. Design-build describes a project delivery method, not a selection process, and does not by itself require qualifications-first evaluation. Low-bid procurement selects on price alone, which is the opposite of what the scenario describes. Sole-source procurement bypasses competitive evaluation entirely.
  3. The correct answer is C (Identify the required disciplines, then solicit qualifications and negotiate consultant scopes and fees). Assembling an appropriate design team begins with defining which disciplines the project genuinely needs, then vetting consultant qualifications and negotiating scopes so responsibilities and fees are clear before design work proceeds. Starting construction documents without needed expertise risks costly errors and rework. Shifting technical responsibility to client staff who were not hired for that role creates liability and coordination gaps. Delaying consultant selection until permitting is far too late for disciplines like structural and civil engineering that shape early design decisions.
  4. The correct answers are A and B: The project has encountered unforeseen site conditions requiring additional investigation and design work, and the client has requested a significant scope expansion not contemplated in the original agreement. A supplemental services amendment is appropriate when the client adds scope beyond the original agreement or when genuinely unforeseen conditions require extra work outside the negotiated scope. Billing for work already covered by the original fee is not a legitimate basis for additional compensation. Correcting an internal quality control failure is the firm's own responsibility and should not be passed on to the client as billable supplemental work.
  5. The correct answer is A (Decisions made later in overlapping phases may force costly rework of earlier phase deliverables). Compressing sequential design phases increases the risk that decisions made during a later phase, such as construction documents, will conflict with or invalidate work already completed in an earlier phase like schematic design, forcing rework and eroding the schedule gain. Compressing the schedule does not reduce billed hours or the client's fee obligation. Client review remains necessary regardless of how phases overlap. Schedule compression has no bearing on whether a project is rebid to competing firms.
  6. The correct answer is C (A work plan breaking the scope into tasks with estimated hours by staff level). A detailed work plan that decomposes the scope into discrete tasks and assigns estimated hours by staff classification is the standard tool for building a defensible fee proposal, since it ties cost directly to the actual work required. The client's maintenance budget relates to operations, not the design fee. A marketing brochure showcases past work but provides no basis for estimating hours on the current project. An outdated generic fee schedule lacks the specificity needed for an accurate, project-tailored estimate.
  7. The correct answer is B (Evaluate budget and schedule impacts, then discuss scope and fee changes with the client). Responsible scope management requires evaluating how a significant addition affects budget and schedule and then communicating those impacts transparently to the client before proceeding, since a mid-project addition typically warrants a fee and schedule amendment. Silently proceeding without analysis risks budget overruns. Flatly refusing without discussion is not collaborative and forecloses a reasonable negotiation. Quietly cutting other elements to absorb cost without telling the client is a breach of the client's trust and design intent.
  8. The correct answer is A (Identify value engineering options or funding adjustments and present them to the client). When bids exceed the budget, the landscape architect's role is to analyze the overage, develop realistic options such as value engineering substitutions or alternate funding sources, and present those choices to the client for a decision. Bid overages have real cost consequences and cannot simply be ignored. Terminating a contractor without proper review is a legal and procedural overreaction unrelated to budget management. A full redesign without client input skips the collaborative process the client is entitled to.
  9. The correct answer is D (To structure deliverables, review points, and payment milestones throughout the project timeline). Dividing an agreement into standard phases establishes clear deliverables, client review checkpoints, and payment milestones tied to progress, which keeps the project organized and the fee aligned with work completed. Phasing is not a mechanism for inflating fees independent of actual work. It does not remove the design team's coordination responsibilities during construction administration. A phased structure still requires, rather than replaces, a clearly written scope of services.
  10. The correct answer is A (Relevant technical experience, licensure, availability, and a record of reliable coordination). Selecting sub-consultants should weigh demonstrated technical experience on comparable projects, appropriate licensure, realistic availability to meet the schedule, and a track record of coordinating well with design teams, since these factors most directly affect project quality and risk. Choosing primarily on lowest fee can compromise quality and create hidden costs later. A recent personal encounter is not a substitute for vetted qualifications. Contract length has little bearing on a consultant's competence or fit for the project.
  11. The correct answers are A, B, and C: the specific tasks and deliverables the firm will provide during each phase, the basis of compensation and payment schedule tied to project milestones, and the services and responsibilities that are explicitly excluded from the agreement. A clear scope of services defines what work is included, how the firm will be paid and when, and what is excluded so both parties share the same expectations and disputes over additional services are minimized. Personal contact information for staff is an administrative detail, not a defining element of contractual scope.
  12. The correct answer is C (Construction administration, which occurs during the contractor's building of the project). Reviewing submittals, responding to requests for information, and conducting site observation reports are core duties of the construction administration phase, when the landscape architect monitors the contractor's work for general conformance with the construction documents. Programming and site analysis occurs earlier and involves gathering data, not reviewing submittals. Schematic design produces early concept alternatives, not construction oversight. Bidding and negotiation ends once a construction contract is executed, before these activities occur.
  13. The correct answer is A (A not-to-exceed cap that limits total fees without requiring a fixed final scope). Adding a not-to-exceed provision to an hourly contract preserves flexibility for a scope that is still evolving while giving the client a firm ceiling on total cost, directly addressing the stated concern. A stipulated lump sum requires the scope to be defined up front, which contradicts the described uncertainty. A verbal agreement with no written cost terms offers no protection to either party and is poor professional practice. An uncapped cost-plus-percentage fee provides no cost predictability at all.
  14. The correct answer is A (It allows the team to set realistic client expectations and sequence dependent tasks accurately). Explicitly accounting for agency review periods, which are often outside the design team's control, lets the team set realistic expectations with the client and correctly sequence tasks that depend on approvals, such as advertising for bids only after permits are issued. Building in review time does not guarantee approval outcomes. The landscape architect still must respond to any agency comments that arise. Scheduling milestones does not eliminate the ongoing need to actively manage and update the schedule.
  15. The correct answer is C (Flag the clause for negotiation, since means and methods are the contractor's responsibility). Construction means, methods, techniques, and sequences are standard contractor responsibilities, so an indemnification clause assigning that liability to the landscape architect should be flagged and negotiated to reflect appropriate risk allocation. Indemnification language is frequently negotiable and should not be signed as-is when it misallocates risk. Such clauses have real legal and financial consequences and cannot be disregarded. Walking away entirely is an overreaction when the issue can typically be resolved through negotiation.
  16. The correct answer is D (The landscape architect often works as a consultant to the design-build entity or contractor). In design-build delivery, a single entity, frequently led by a contractor, holds the overall contract with the owner, and the landscape architect commonly participates as a consultant to that design-build entity rather than contracting separately and independently with the owner. Direct, independent owner contracts are more characteristic of design-bid-build delivery. Landscape architects remain actively involved through later phases, not just schematic design. There is no prohibition against landscape architects participating in design-build projects.
  17. The correct answer is A (Decisions and direction given verbally may later be disputed without a written record). Without written meeting minutes, decisions, approvals, and direction given verbally can later become the subject of disagreement or dispute, since there is no documented record confirming what was discussed or authorized. Verbal agreements are not automatically enforceable and are far harder to prove than written records. Skipping meeting minutes has no effect on the validity of the underlying signed agreement. Failing to document meetings does not shift responsibility for errors onto the client.
  18. The correct answers are A and B: building a critical path that identifies which tasks directly control the overall completion date, and updating the schedule regularly as actual progress and agency review timelines become known. Effective schedule management identifies the critical path of interdependent tasks and treats the schedule as a living document that is updated as real progress and external factors, like agency review times, become clearer. Refusing to revise a schedule after kickoff ignores real project conditions and invites missed deadlines. Withholding schedule information from consultants undermines coordination rather than supporting it.
  19. The correct answer is B (The fee may fluctuate as construction cost estimates change, creating uncertainty for both parties). Because a percentage-of-construction-cost fee is tied directly to an estimate that can change as the design develops and bids come in, both the firm and the client face some uncertainty about the final fee amount until construction costs are finalized. This fee method is not legally barred from public projects and is used on many of them. It does not inherently produce a lower total fee than other methods, since the outcome depends on the actual construction cost. Deliverables and time tracking are still typically required regardless of fee structure.
  20. The correct answer is C (Professional liability insurance, which covers claims arising from errors in professional services). Professional liability, also called errors and omissions insurance, specifically covers claims alleging negligent acts, errors, or omissions in the performance of professional design services, which is exactly the risk described. General liability covers bodily injury and property damage rather than design errors. Automobile insurance covers vehicle-related claims and is unrelated to design negligence. Workers compensation covers employee injuries on the job, not claims about the quality of professional services.
  21. The correct answer is D (Discuss the request with the client, clarifying that reduced fee may require reduced scope). A professionally sound response is to have an open conversation with the client explaining that fee and scope are linked, so a fee reduction without a corresponding scope reduction is generally not sustainable, and to negotiate an outcome both parties understand. Agreeing without discussion risks the firm doing full scope work for inadequate compensation. Ceasing communication and stopping all work abruptly is an extreme response that breaches the professional relationship. Secretly reducing quality without informing the client is dishonest and exposes the firm to liability.
  22. The correct answer is C (That all contracted deliverables and phases have been completed and properly documented). Before issuing a final invoice, the project manager should confirm that every deliverable and phase in the contract has actually been completed and documented, ensuring the invoice accurately reflects the work performed and reducing the chance of billing disputes. The status of the client's unrelated projects has no bearing on this project's final invoice. Staffing decisions within the firm are unrelated to invoicing. Notifying competing firms is not a standard or relevant closeout task.
  23. The correct answer is B (The agency and firm continue good-faith fee negotiations before moving to another qualified firm). Under QBS, if fee negotiations with the top-ranked, most-qualified firm are unsuccessful after good-faith efforts, the agency may end negotiations with that firm and begin negotiating with the next most qualified firm, rather than immediately reopening the process to price competition. The firm is not obligated to accept an inadequate offer merely to preserve future work. QBS does not convert into a low-bid process at this stage. Firms are not permanently barred from fee negotiation under QBS procedures.
  24. The correct answer is D (Delineated flood zones and base flood elevations used to assess flood risk on the site). A Flood Insurance Rate Map, issued by FEMA, delineates flood hazard zones such as the 100-year floodplain and provides base flood elevations, which are essential inputs for assessing flood risk and determining design and permitting requirements near a riverside site. Zoning classification comes from municipal zoning maps and ordinances, not the FIRM. Ownership history and easements are found in title reports, not flood maps. Soil classification and percolation data come from geotechnical or soil surveys, not FEMA mapping.
  25. The correct answers are A and B: confirming the project falls under NPDES construction general permit coverage before land disturbance begins, and identifying the SWPPP requirements the design and construction team must satisfy once coverage is obtained. Early in inventory and pre-design, the landscape architect should confirm whether the project needs NPDES construction general permit coverage for its disturbed acreage and identify what a resulting Stormwater Pollution Prevention Plan will require of the design and construction team. The one-acre disturbance threshold, not adjacency to existing pavement, determines whether coverage applies, so that assumption is incorrect. A federal NPDES permit does not substitute for separately applicable local municipal stormwater ordinance requirements, which must still be reviewed.
  26. The correct answer is B (Point B): Point B sits in the open southern portion of the site, clear of the building footprint, the driveway, and both existing trees, giving the widest unobstructed sightline across the property for a repeatable reference photograph. This location supports the systematic, cross-referenced documentation approach a professional field record depends on for later analysis, design decisions, and permitting. Point A sits close enough to the building to have its view partially blocked by the structure itself. Point C sits near the driveway where parked or moving vehicles would routinely obstruct the frame, and Point D sits at the opposite corner of the site from Point B, tucked into a corner where the building and driveway both cut across the sightline back toward the rest of the property.
  27. The correct answer is D (GPS provides georeferenced coordinates that can be directly imported into mapping software). A handheld GPS unit records accurate, georeferenced coordinates for field-observed features that can be directly imported into GIS or CAD software, improving accuracy and efficiency compared to hand-sketched locations that must be manually transcribed and are prone to error. GPS data collection still requires an in-person site visit to record. GPS units capture location, not species identification, which still requires field observation or expertise. A GPS-based inventory does not substitute for a licensed boundary survey required for legal purposes.
  28. The correct answer is B (It establishes long-range community goals and land use policy that inform project context). A municipal comprehensive plan lays out long-range community goals, land use policy, and growth strategy, giving the landscape architect essential context about how a project fits within broader planning objectives and future development patterns. It is a policy document, not a source of construction specifications. Alignment with the comprehensive plan does not guarantee automatic project approval, since separate review and approval processes still apply. The comprehensive plan complements rather than replaces review of the specific zoning ordinance governing the parcel.
  29. The correct answer is A (The NPDES program administered under the Clean Water Act for construction stormwater discharge). The National Pollutant Discharge Elimination System, established under the Clean Water Act and administered by EPA or delegated state agencies, requires a permit for stormwater discharges from construction sites disturbing one acre or more, typically implemented through a SWPPP. NEPA governs environmental review for federal actions generally, not specifically construction stormwater. The National Historic Preservation Act addresses historic resources, not stormwater discharge. The Uniform Relocation Assistance Act concerns property acquisition and relocation, unrelated to stormwater permitting.
  30. The correct answer is A (Silt fence installed along the downslope perimeter of disturbed areas). Silt fence is a standard, widely used erosion and sediment control best management practice that intercepts sheet flow and traps sediment along the downslope edge of a disturbed area before it can leave the site. Removing all vegetation prematurely increases, rather than controls, erosion risk. Directing untreated runoff straight into a storm drain violates the purpose of sediment control and typical permit conditions. Erosion controls must be installed before grading begins, not after, to be effective.
  31. The correct answer is B (The design must accommodate the easement, which may restrict structures or plantings within it). A recorded easement grants specific rights, such as utility access, over a defined portion of the property, and the design must accommodate those rights, which commonly restrict permanent structures, deep-rooted trees, or grade changes within the easement area. Easements can affect many types of rights, not only mineral rights. An easement grants a right of use, not a transfer of underlying ownership. Removing a recorded easement requires a formal legal process, not a simple request from the landscape architect.
  32. The correct answers are A and B: the applicable local zoning ordinance governing permitted uses and dimensional standards, and the site's current FEMA Flood Insurance Rate Map showing flood hazard zones. Collecting the governing zoning ordinance and the current flood hazard mapping are standard, directly relevant steps in policy and regulatory research because both shape what can be built on the site and where. Adjacent owners' personal home addresses are not a regulatory document and raise privacy concerns beyond project need. A comprehensive or master plan from an unrelated jurisdiction has no bearing on the subject site's regulatory context.
  33. The correct answer is A (Point A): Point A sits along the site's northwest edge, directly adjacent to the abutting commercial building identified during the land use inventory, making it the logical location to concentrate screening and buffering plantings that respond to that adjacency. Identifying this adjacent land use early lets the design address compatibility, buffering, access points, and potential conflicts before schematic design proceeds. Point B sits along the same northern edge but farther from the commercial building's actual frontage, so it is a secondary rather than primary buffering location. Points C and D sit along the site's southern edge, near the driveway and away from the commercial adjacency the buffer needs to address.
  34. The correct answer is B (Point B): Point B sits at the southeast corner of the site, close to the driveway entrance and with a clear sightline back toward the building facade, making it the vantage point that captures both features in one documented photograph. A systematically logged photo record cross-referenced by location and view direction like this creates a reliable visual record the whole design team can reference during analysis and later review. Points A and D sit on the opposite, northwest side of the site, too far from the driveway to include it in the same frame. Point C sits near the building but on the side away from the driveway, so it would miss the vehicular access point entirely.
  35. The correct answer is C (The 811 call-before-you-dig program that locates buried utilities before excavation). Calling 811 before excavation triggers the nationally recognized one-call notification system, which requires affected utility owners to mark the approximate location of buried lines before digging begins, reducing the risk of utility strikes, injury, and service disruption. This process carries real legal significance in most jurisdictions, and utilities are generally obligated to respond within a set timeframe. It applies broadly to excavation activity, not only residential lots. The call must be made before, not after, excavation to be effective.
  36. The correct answer is C (Flag the area and recommend a formal wetland delineation by a qualified professional). Observing indicators such as hydric soils, hydrophytic vegetation, and prolonged saturation suggests a possible jurisdictional wetland, and the appropriate response is to flag the area for a formal delineation by a qualified wetland scientist, since only that process legally confirms wetland boundaries and jurisdiction. Preliminary indicators should prompt investigation rather than being dismissed and left for an agency to discover unprompted. Filling or grading a suspected wetland before delineation and permitting can create serious legal and environmental liability. Wetland presence has direct and significant implications for site design and permitting.
  37. The correct answer is B (Note the discrepancy and recommend the client obtain a licensed boundary survey to resolve it). A discrepancy between a recorded plat and an observed fence line should be documented and referred to a licensed surveyor, since determining the legal boundary is outside the landscape architect's scope of authority and requires a professional survey to resolve reliably. Assuming the fence is correct without verification risks designing on the wrong boundary. Fences frequently do not align with legal property lines, so the two cannot be assumed very similar. Physically relocating a fence is both outside the landscape architect's role and could create legal liability.
  38. The correct answer is C (Grading and stormwater design decisions based on how water currently moves across the site). Recording existing slopes, drainage patterns, and evidence of ponding after rainfall directly informs grading and stormwater management design by revealing how water currently behaves on site, which the design must either preserve, redirect, or mitigate. This field data has no direct bearing on furniture finish selection. Legal boundaries are established through survey and title research, not drainage observation. Marketing materials are unrelated to physical site investigation findings.
  39. The correct answers are A and B: cross-referenced photo logs noting location, direction, and date for each image taken, and handheld GPS coordinates recorded for significant existing features observed in the field. Systematically logged photographs and georeferenced GPS points together create an accurate, verifiable record of existing conditions that supports later analysis and design decisions. Relying on unaided memory long after a site visit introduces significant risk of error and omission. Photographs alone, without accompanying written notes on measurements, materials, and context, do not capture the complete picture a design team needs.
  40. The correct answer is A (Permitted uses, setback requirements, height limits, and lot coverage standards for the parcel). A zoning ordinance governs land use regulation, including which uses are permitted on a parcel, required setbacks from property lines, maximum height limits, and lot coverage standards, all of which directly constrain what a community garden project can include. Plant species recommendations come from horticultural or ecological references, not zoning code. Irrigation piping specifications are technical construction details, not zoning content. Ownership history is found in title records, not the zoning ordinance.
  41. The correct answer is A (Document the extent of exposed bedrock and recommend geotechnical investigation for the area). Exposed bedrock can significantly affect excavation feasibility, grading design, and construction cost, so the appropriate response is to document its extent during inventory and recommend a geotechnical investigation to characterize subsurface conditions before design proceeds further. Bedrock has substantial cost and feasibility implications and should not be disregarded. Assuming easy, low-cost removal without geotechnical data is an unfounded and risky assumption. Redesigning without gathering additional technical information skips a necessary analysis step.
  42. The correct answer is B (They document the approximate location and type of existing infrastructure to inform design and coordination). As-built drawings record the approximate location, depth, and type of previously installed infrastructure, giving the design team an early basis for coordinating new design elements with existing utilities and identifying potential conflicts before construction documents are prepared. As-builts document infrastructure, not legal property boundaries. Because as-built records can be outdated or inaccurate, they do not eliminate the separate requirement to call 811 before excavation. As-built information is most valuable early in the process, not only after construction documents are finalized.
  43. The correct answer is D (Significant existing trees should be inventoried and evaluated against the ordinance's protection criteria). Discovering a tree preservation ordinance means the landscape architect should inventory trees meeting or exceeding the protected caliper threshold and evaluate the design against the ordinance's permitting and preservation requirements, since failing to do so risks violations and delays. Tree preservation ordinances frequently apply to private development, contrary to this option. Removing protected trees to avoid a known ordinance is both unethical and likely illegal. Many tree ordinances apply to private property, not primarily public land, so that assumption is incorrect.
  44. The correct answer is B (General contextual observations of surrounding character, scale, land use, and access patterns). A windshield survey is a quick, informal drive-through or walk-through reconnaissance used to gather general contextual impressions such as neighborhood character, building scale, surrounding land use, traffic patterns, and access points, providing useful early context rather than precise technical data. Legal boundary coordinates require a licensed survey, not a windshield survey. Geotechnical bearing capacity data requires soil borings and laboratory testing. Certified utility depths require as-built records or field verification, not a general drive-through observation.
  45. The correct answer is B (It grounds the proposed design in an understanding of the area's history, character, and prior planning intent). Reviewing historic photographs, prior planning studies, and existing design guidelines helps the landscape architect understand the area's evolution, established character, and prior planning intent, which grounds new design decisions in genuine contextual knowledge rather than assumptions. No amount of research guarantees unanimous public approval of a design. Contextual research supplements, rather than replaces, direct field verification through site visits. Reviewing historical context does not substitute for confirming current, applicable zoning and design standards.
  46. The correct answers are A and B: verifying existing conditions shown on available base maps and aerial imagery in the field, and recording observed vegetation, soils, drainage, and structures not visible in remote data sources. Onsite fieldwork exists to ground-truth remote data sources and to capture conditions, such as understory vegetation or seasonal drainage patterns, that aerial imagery and existing maps cannot fully convey. Available imagery does not eliminate the value of an in-person visit, since imagery can be outdated or incomplete. Fieldwork supports data collection, but final design decisions still require subsequent analysis rather than being made on the spot.
  47. The correct answer is C (Research the specific coastal zone requirements and permitting agencies that apply to the site). Discovering that a site falls within a coastal zone management area means the landscape architect should research the specific applicable requirements, review agencies, and permitting processes that govern development in that zone, since these often add layers of regulation beyond standard local zoning. Coastal zone programs frequently apply to private development, not solely federal agency actions. Assuming no additional permitting will be required ignores a documented regulatory trigger. Relocating a project boundary without verification is an unfounded assumption that could still leave the site within the regulated zone.
  48. The correct answer is C (To structure raw data so it can be systematically analyzed for opportunities and constraints). Organizing inventory findings into consistent categories such as topography, hydrology, vegetation, soils, and infrastructure creates a structured foundation that supports systematic opportunities and constraints analysis, helping the design team identify patterns and relationships across the data. Padding a report for its own sake is not a legitimate professional purpose. Organized inventory data is a precursor to analysis, not a substitute that eliminates the need for it. The organizational structure serves an analytical function, not merely an aesthetic one.
  49. The correct answer is B (811 locates mark approximate field locations near excavation time, not detailed design-phase records). The 811 system exists to mark approximate utility locations shortly before excavation begins, but it does not provide the detailed record drawings or long-range planning data useful earlier in design, which is why contacting individual utilities directly for as-built records remains valuable during the design phase. 811 field marking is not a substitute for detailed record drawings. 811 services are broadly available to anyone planning excavation, not restricted to landscape architects. Direct utility outreach remains a separate, useful step and is not eliminated by the existence of 811.
  50. The correct answer is D (Potential soil or groundwater contamination should be flagged for further environmental investigation). Evidence of historic fuel storage in a Phase I assessment is a recognized environmental concern that should be flagged for further investigation, typically a Phase II assessment, since contamination can affect grading, planting, stormwater design, and construction safety on the site. Phase I findings are directly relevant to landscape design and are not irrelevant to the discipline. Beginning excavation without further investigation of a documented contamination risk is unsafe and potentially illegal. Contamination concerns can affect soils, plantings, and site grading, not only above-ground building construction.
  51. The correct answer is A (Point A): Point A sits at the northwest corner of the site plan, opposite the paved walkway that runs along the southern edge, which is exactly where a diagonal desire line worn into the open lawn would originate if pedestrians are cutting across to avoid the longer paved route. Points B, C, and D sit along the eastern and southern edges of the plan, closer to the paved walkway itself, so none of them mark the same bypass pattern described in the field observation. Documenting the worn path at its true origin lets the design team evaluate paving that diagonal as a formal route. Field-verified desire lines are strong evidence for where circulation should actually go, not just where it was originally planned.
  52. The correct answer is B (Research applicable historic preservation review requirements before finalizing design concepts). Discovering National Register listing means the landscape architect should research the applicable historic preservation review process, which may involve state historic preservation offices or local landmark commissions, before finalizing design concepts that could affect character-defining features. National Register status can trigger review requirements, particularly where federal funding, permits, or licenses are involved, so it should not be disregarded. Removing features without preservation consultation risks violating applicable review requirements. Historic preservation review frequently extends to exterior features and landscape elements, not just building interiors.
  53. The correct answers are A and B: it compiles verified existing conditions data such as topography, boundaries, and infrastructure onto one drawing, and it provides a common reference the design team and consultants can build design layers upon. A base map assembles verified existing conditions data into a single coordinated drawing that becomes the shared foundation the design team and consultants use for developing design layers. A base map is a working document that is updated as more data becomes available, not a permanent lock on future information. A base map is a technical drawing, not a substitute for the legal professional services agreement.
  54. The correct answer is C (General soil series classifications, drainage characteristics, and suitability ratings for the area). A Natural Resources Conservation Service soil survey provides mapped soil series with general characteristics such as drainage class, permeability, and suitability ratings for various uses, offering a useful starting point for planting and site planning decisions. It does not provide real-time moisture monitoring, which requires field instrumentation. Legal boundaries and easements come from surveys and title records, not soil surveys. NRCS data is general and not a substitute for site-specific geotechnical engineering data needed for structural foundation design.
  55. The correct answer is C (The slopes show signs of instability and likely require erosion control and stabilization measures). Exposed roots, active erosion, and rill formation are visible indicators of slope instability and ongoing soil loss, directly supporting the conclusion that the design should incorporate erosion control and stabilization measures on those slopes. These observations indicate instability, the opposite of stability. Erosion indicators on a hillside are unrelated to wetland hydrology indicators such as hydric soils and saturation. Erosion evidence has no bearing on determining the legal property boundary.
  56. The correct answer is D (The Americans with Disabilities Act accessibility guidelines for pedestrian rights-of-way). Recording sidewalk widths, curb ramp locations, and cross slopes directly supports evaluating existing pedestrian infrastructure against ADA accessibility guidelines, which set specific standards for clear widths, running and cross slopes, and curb ramp design in the public right-of-way. The National Historic Preservation Act addresses historic resource review, not accessibility dimensions. The Endangered Species Act concerns protected species and habitat, unrelated to sidewalk geometry. The Uniform Relocation Assistance Act governs property acquisition and relocation assistance, not accessibility standards.
  57. The correct answer is B (Point B): Point B sits along the site's northeast edge, the stretch of the plan the scenario places along the stream corridor, so it is the correct location to flag the ordinary high water mark before it is used to establish the jurisdictional wetland boundary. Points A, C, and D sit along the western and southern portions of the plan, away from the stream corridor described in the scenario, so flagging the mark there would not reflect where the actual watercourse runs. The ordinary high water mark is a physical, field-verified line, not an assumed setback, and it directly determines what permitting requirements apply to work near the corridor. Getting this location right protects the project from later jurisdictional disputes.
  58. The correct answer is C (Point C): Point C sits immediately adjacent to both the existing building and the driveway, so a proposed hardscape expansion at that location would compound the site's existing impervious surface the fastest, making it the area the design team should track most closely against the forty percent zoning limit. Once an impervious coverage limit is identified, the design team should track cumulative building footprint, paving, and hardscape coverage against that limit throughout the design process. Points A and B sit well away from the existing building and driveway, so hardscape added there would be easier to offset with open space elsewhere on the site. Point D sits in an open corner of the site with no existing impervious surface nearby to compound.
  59. The correct answer is D (It offers population, age, and household characteristics that inform program and design decisions). Census demographic data provides population size, age distribution, household composition, and other socioeconomic characteristics of the surrounding community, informing decisions about park programming, amenities, and design that will serve the actual population. Census data does not provide topographic elevation, which requires survey information. It has no bearing on legal parcel boundaries. Demographic data supplements, but does not replace, direct public engagement with area residents about their own preferences.
  60. The correct answers are A and B: hand auger borings to observe soil profile, texture, and approximate depth to groundwater, and coordinating with a geotechnical engineer for deeper test pits or borings when warranted. Shallow hand auger borings performed by the landscape architect or a soil scientist can reveal general soil profile and drainage characteristics, while more significant structural or depth questions warrant coordinating with a geotechnical engineer for formal test pits or borings. Surface vegetation color is not a valid method for estimating bearing capacity. Assuming uniform subsurface conditions without any sampling contradicts the purpose of conducting a soil investigation.
  61. The correct answer is A (Calculate pre- and post-development runoff rates and design detention or other controls accordingly). Complying with a local stormwater ordinance that caps post-development runoff at pre-development rates requires the design team to calculate both conditions for the specified storm event and design detention, retention, or other stormwater management measures sized to meet that standard. Local stormwater ordinances are not automatically superseded by federal law and must still be followed alongside any applicable federal permitting. Simply including landscaping does not automatically achieve a specific engineered runoff rate requirement. Local ordinances typically apply within the municipality's jurisdiction, the opposite of applying only outside it.
  62. The correct answer is B (To identify constraints and opportunities related to grading, accessibility, and erosion potential). The firm's internal staffing structure has no bearing on the commission's review of resource impacts. A history of unrelated projects in other states does not inform the commission's evaluation of this specific project's environmental effects.
  120. The correct answer is B (Explore additional outreach methods, such as different meeting times, locations, or digital tools, to reach underrepresented groups). Recognizing an engagement gap among younger residents and renters should prompt exploring additional or different outreach approaches, such as varied meeting times, alternative locations, or digital participation tools, aimed at reducing barriers and reaching a more representative cross-section of the community. Assuming disinterest without further investigation is an unfounded conclusion that overlooks likely structural barriers to attendance, such as scheduling or venue accessibility. Continuing an approach already shown to produce an unrepresentative turnout is unlikely to change the outcome. Deliberately narrowing future outreach to only the groups already engaged would worsen, not correct, the representation gap.

Satisfying a documented requirement for fire department coordination involves directly engaging the fire department about emergency vehicle access at this specific entrance, not assuming adequacy without confirmation. Adding an online comment option extends genuine participation opportunities to residents who cannot attend in-person meetings because of work schedules, caregiving responsibilities, mobility limitations, or geographic distance along a regional trail corridor, broadening the overall reach of engagement. Online tools typically supplement rather than eliminate the value of in-person meetings, which support different kinds of interaction. Providing an online comment option does not remove the design team's responsibility to review and meaningfully consider the comments received through any channel.
  123. The correct answers are A and B: documented public input can strengthen a project's case for approval by demonstrating community support and responsiveness, and governing bodies often expect evidence of adequate public engagement as part of their formal review process. Well-documented public engagement can help demonstrate community support and a responsive design process, and many governing bodies explicitly expect evidence of adequate engagement as part of what they consider during formal review. Public participation improves, but does not guarantee, an automatic approval outcome, since governing bodies weigh many additional factors. Many formal approval processes do explicitly consider whether adequate public engagement occurred, contrary to this option.

A poorly graded sand with essentially no fines would be classified SP.

The two areas are not equally suitable, since the well-drained deep water table area is generally more favorable for structures.   155. The correct answers are A and C: The Unified Soil Classification System uses a first symbol for the dominant soil type, such as G for gravel, S for sand, M for silt, or C for clay, and a second symbol that describes characteristics like gradation, for example W for well-graded or P for poorly graded, or plasticity for fine-grained soils. The system is based on grain size distribution and plasticity, not soil color. USCS addresses geotechnical engineering properties and does not test for chemical contaminants.
  156. This is precisely the type of condition that requires special geotechnical input, not standard grading assumptions. It clearly represents a constraint rather than a site free of limitations. Slope stability concerns are unrelated to prime farmland classification.
  157. The correct answer is B (The soil formed under conditions of prolonged saturation and may indicate wetland presence): A hydric soil designation indicates the soil developed under prolonged saturation, ponding, or flooding, which is one of the three parameters used to identify potential wetlands and should prompt further wetland delineation. Hydric status is unrelated to prime farmland classification, which uses separate criteria. Hydric soils are typically poorly suited for bearing structural loads due to their saturated, often organic nature. A hydric soil designation does not confirm anything about floodplain boundaries, which are determined by FEMA mapping.

The designation is not limited to parcels in active agricultural use. It does not typically prohibit development, though it may add a review step. This consideration applies to the project generally, not only if a stormwater pond is included.

Zone 1's organic peat is designated with an O suffix such as OL or Pt, not ML.

This is not a cosmetic issue since ongoing erosion can worsen and cause sediment problems downstream. The stable, vegetated zones (1, 2, and 4) show no comparable erosion indicators and do not require this stabilization response at this time.
  162. The correct answers are A and D: The Web Soil Survey provides mapped soil unit boundaries plus interpretive data such as drainage class, and it reports depth to water table and depth to restrictive features, both useful for early constraints and opportunities mapping. It is a planning-level tool, not a legally binding wetland delineation, which still requires field verification by a qualified professional. It also does not replace project-specific geotechnical borings needed to confirm engineering properties for structures.

Zone 2's excessively drained sandy loam drains too quickly and is better suited to drought-tolerant, xeric plantings rather than a rain garden. Zones 1 and 4 have no notable drainage contrast documented and were not identified as offering a differentiated design opportunity. Adjacent map units frequently differ in drainage class and should not be assumed identical when siting stormwater features.

A thin unreinforced section directly on expansive clay would be prone to premature cracking and heaving. Expansive clay affects pavements as well as vegetation, contrary to the claim that it only affects planting. This condition increases, not eliminates, the need for a geotechnical pavement recommendation.
  165. The correct answer is C (The area is likely a wetland or seasonally saturated zone constraining conventional development): Very poorly drained soils with organic surface layers are strongly associated with wetland or seasonally saturated conditions, a significant constraint on conventional development that should prompt wetland delineation. A high water table and organic surface soils are poor conditions for a below-grade structure, not ideal ones. Naturally occurring organic surface layers are not evidence of contamination. This soil description says nothing about coastal high hazard area boundaries, which are determined by separate coastal flood mapping.
  166. The correct answer is B (As a significant constraint generally unsuitable for development without substantial engineering): Slopes in the 15 percent and greater range are widely treated as a significant constraint, generally unsuitable for conventional development without substantial engineering such as retaining walls, terracing, or specialized foundations. This range is not considered prime buildable area under typical slope-based suitability guidelines. Steep slope does not automatically require stormwater detention, which is sited based on drainage design, not just slope percentage. Steep slopes clearly require different design treatment than flat land due to erosion, access, and grading challenges.
  167. The correct answer is A (Zone 1): Slopes between 0 and 5 percent are generally the most favorable for building placement, parking, and other intensive uses, requiring minimal cut and fill. Zone 2, with slopes exceeding 15 percent, presents a significant constraint requiring costly engineering and is generally avoided for building footprints due to cost and stability concerns. Zones 3 and 4, at moderate 5 to 10 percent slopes, are workable for some site elements but are less favorable than Zone 1 for the primary building footprint.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (To identify areas of relative development suitability and potential erosion or grading challenges): Banding slope into standard percentage ranges helps identify which portions of the site are more suitable for development versus which present grading, erosion, or accessibility challenges, directly supporting the constraints and opportunities analysis. Stormwater detention volume requires hydrologic calculations beyond a slope band map alone. Floodplain boundaries come from FEMA mapping, not slope analysis. A GIS-derived slope analysis is a planning tool and does not replace a certified topographic survey needed for construction documents.
  2. The correct answer is D (Zone 4): Closely spaced contour lines indicate a steep grade change over a short horizontal distance, signaling a relatively steep area that can pose challenges for accessible routes, erosion control, and construction. Zones 1, 2, and 3, with widely spaced contours, indicate flatter terrain suitable for accessible routes without major regrading. Contour spacing reflects slope, not floodplain status or soil drainage class, which require separate regulatory and soils data respectively.
  3. The correct answer is A (Zone 1): A steep section exceeding 12 percent along the shortest alignment would force the path well beyond standard accessible running slope limits, requiring switchbacks, ramps, or a longer route to meet accessibility requirements. Zone 3's slopes consistently under 3 percent, Zone 4's level plateau, and Zone 2's minor undulations under 2 percent are all conditions that support, rather than challenge, an accessible route.
  4. The correct answer is C (Whether the pad location or elevation can be adjusted to reduce earthwork volume and cost): Significant cut and fill volumes signal an opportunity to reconsider pad location, orientation, or finished floor elevation to better fit existing topography and reduce earthwork cost and disturbance. Cut and fill volumes have no bearing on farmland classification, which is a soils-based designation. Grading needs do not redefine a wetland boundary, which is based on hydrology, soils, and vegetation criteria. Significant cut and fill is common on real projects and does not by itself justify eliminating the program.
  5. The correct answer is D (An opportunity to rely on positive drainage away from the foundation with minimal regrading): Grades sloping away from the foundation on all sides indicate favorable positive drainage already exists, which is an opportunity to preserve that pattern with minimal regrading in the design. This condition does not necessitate relocating the building, since positive drainage away from a foundation is generally desirable, not problematic. A general slope-away condition does not by itself trigger the need for a formal slope stability study, which is reserved for steep or unstable conditions. Positive drainage away from a foundation says nothing about floodway designation.
  6. The correct answers are B and C: Slopes between 10 and 15 percent are commonly treated as a moderate constraint that calls for careful grading and erosion control measures, and accessible routes crossing this range typically need ramps, switchbacks, or additional grading to meet running slope limits. This range is not treated as prime buildable land without special measures, since it presents real design challenges. Slope percentage alone says nothing about depth to bedrock, which requires separate geotechnical investigation.
  7. The correct answer is B (A depression or basin condition that may require an engineered drainage solution): A low point with no natural outlet is a depression or basin condition that will collect runoff and likely requires an engineered solution such as a storm drain, dry well, or constructed swale to convey water off site. Every site lies within some watershed, so this finding does not place the site outside a watershed boundary. Topographic depression is unrelated to farmland classification, which is soils based. A depression with no outlet is more likely, not less likely, to experience standing water without intervention.
  8. The correct answer is C (It indicates the site straddles a drainage divide, meaning it contributes to two distinct watersheds): A ridge line with drainage flowing in opposite directions indicates the site straddles a drainage divide and contributes runoff to two distinct watersheds, an important consideration for stormwater management and downstream impact analysis. This is the opposite of a single shared watershed, since the two sides drain separately. Ridge lines directly affect stormwater routing, not just visual character, so this has real hydrologic significance. A ridge line by itself does not render a site unbuildable.
  9. The correct answer is A (The potential need to maintain or replace the drainage function to avoid impacting the neighboring property): Filling a swale that conveys off-site drainage could create or worsen flooding on the neighboring property, so the analysis should flag the need to maintain or replace that conveyance function, potentially through a redesigned drainage system. The swale's function is directly relevant even though it partly serves an adjacent property. A swale is not typically a jurisdictional stream simply because it conveys runoff. Not every swale modification triggers a floodplain permit, since that depends on whether the swale lies within a mapped floodplain.
  10. The correct answer is C (It informs how planters, pavers, and drainage on the terrace integrate with existing roof drains): Understanding the roof's existing slope and drain locations allows the landscape architect to design pavers, planters, and any supplemental drainage layers so that water is properly directed to the existing drains rather than pooling or overloading the waterproofing system. This detail is directly relevant to landscape design coordination with the structural and roofing systems, not outside the scope. Roof slope has no relationship to floodplain regulation, which concerns ground-level flood elevations. Roof slope does not by itself dictate plant species selection, which depends on factors like sun, wind exposure, and soil depth.
  11. The correct answer is B (As a potential structural constraint warranting evaluation by a structural or geotechnical engineer): Bulging and cracking in a retaining wall are signs of possible structural distress, and this should be flagged as a potential constraint requiring evaluation by a structural or geotechnical engineer before the design relies on or modifies the wall. This is a structural safety concern, not merely an aesthetic one. Wall distress has no bearing on farmland classification. A retaining wall's existence, let alone its distress, suggests the opposite of universally gentle, easily buildable slopes.
  12. The correct answer is A (As an opportunity area well suited to development given its access, slope, and drainage): Flat, well-drained ground located near the site entrance combines favorable access, gentle slope, and good drainage, making it a strong opportunity area for development within the constraints and opportunities analysis. Proximity to the entrance is generally an advantage for access and visibility, not a reason to avoid the area. Being flat and well drained does not automatically require the area to become detention, which depends on the overall stormwater strategy. Flat, favorable ground is not required to remain undisturbed simply because it is a desirable location.
  13. The correct answers are B and C: Reviewing the effective FEMA Flood Insurance Rate Map is the standard way to identify the extent of the 100-year, or 1 percent annual chance, floodplain, and floodplain boundaries meaningfully influence permitted uses, required finished floor elevations, and fill or grading restrictions. Floodplain mapping applies to riverine and inland flooding, not only coastal sites, so limiting its relevance to coastlines is inaccurate. FEMA regularly issues map revisions based on updated hydrologic and hydraulic data, so floodplain boundaries can and do change over time.

Peak runoff rate changes are a central concern in stormwater design, not something that rarely matters. An increased runoff rate calculation does not establish hydric soil conditions, which require separate soils evidence. Runoff rate findings inform the stormwater strategy, not the specific plant species selected for a project.

A single parameter alone, such as vegetation, is not considered sufficient on its own for a defensible determination, which is precisely why the three-parameter approach exists. The three-parameter method is used broadly for both inland freshwater and coastal wetlands, not limited to coastal areas. Storm surge and riverine flooding are driven by different mechanisms and are not identical phenomena requiring the same analysis. Storm surge risk is broadly relevant to any coastal development type, not limited to agricultural uses. Storm surge data supplements rather than replaces standard FEMA floodplain review, since both are relevant to a coastal site.

Watershed-scale data is genuinely useful for informing an individual site's design and regulatory context, not something to disregard at the individual project scale.

  121. The correct answer is A (To assess and mitigate the project's effects on historic properties before federal approval): Section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act requires federal agencies to consider the effects of federally funded, licensed, or permitted undertakings on historic properties and to consult on ways to avoid, minimize, or mitigate adverse effects. It does not mandate literal replication of a historic layout, exempt the project from other reviews, or transfer property ownership. Ethnographic landscapes are defined by association with contemporary cultural groups.

Beginning construction first, assuming listing is an automatic prohibition, or limiting consultation to the internal design team all misrepresent the required process.
