Simply deleting the amenity without discussion denies the client the chance to weigh phasing or funding options.
  134. The correct answer is C (It ensures the selected amenities remain financially sustainable for the agency to operate over time): Considering operations and maintenance costs during programming helps ensure that proposed amenities are financially sustainable for the managing agency to operate and maintain over the long term, not just affordable to build. It does not replace the capital cost estimate, and it does not guarantee any particular funding source will cover future costs. Addressing O&M early, rather than after construction, is what allows realistic amenity selection.
  135. The correct answer is B (To identify the functional requirements and priorities that will shape the site program): A needs assessment gathers input on user requirements, priorities, and comparable precedents to inform what the program should include, which then guides subsequent site analysis and design. It is not the step where a construction schedule, plant species selection, or grading plan is determined, since those decisions depend on design development that follows programming. Needs assessment establishes the functional foundation for everything that comes after.
  136. The correct answer is D (To account for unforeseen conditions and design refinements as the project develops): A contingency allowance provides a buffer for unforeseen site conditions, market fluctuations, and design refinements that commonly occur as a project moves from concept through construction documents. It does not guarantee the final cost will match the initial estimate exactly, nor does it eliminate the need to update cost estimates as design progresses. It is also not intended to fund amenities that were deliberately left out of the approved program.
  137. The correct answers are C and D: A thorough program documents desired amenities alongside order-of-magnitude cost estimates so trade-offs can be made, and it considers ongoing operations and maintenance so the agency can sustain what gets built. Specifying exact planting species belongs to later design phases once site analysis has established growing conditions, not to the program stage. Programming also does not eliminate future stakeholder engagement, since input continues to shape the plan through subsequent phases.
  138. The correct answer is B (Synthesize the individual data layers into a composite analysis identifying opportunities and constraints): Synthesis combines individual data layers, such as soils, hydrology, vegetation, and slope, into an integrated analysis that reveals patterns, opportunities, and constraints the raw data alone does not show. Discarding the data, forwarding it unprocessed to the client, or delaying interpretation until construction documents undermines the purpose of collecting it in the first place. Synthesis is what turns inventory into an actionable planning tool.
  139. The correct answer is C (Identifying areas best suited for conservation versus areas more suitable for development): Overlaying environmental constraints such as floodplain, steep slope, and mature woodland reveals where development should be avoided and where more resilient, buildable areas exist, directly supporting land use and conservation decisions. This overlay does not determine building square footage, architectural style, or a construction schedule, which depend on programming and design decisions made separately from the environmental constraint analysis.
  140. The correct answer is A (Balance conservation of sensitive resources with carefully limited, low-impact public access): Good synthesis reconciles competing findings by proposing low-impact strategies, such as boardwalks or limited overlooks, that allow appropriate public access while protecting sensitive resources. Prioritizing development on the most sensitive land or ignoring the ecological data undermines the purpose of the analysis. Excluding all recreational use for the entire corridor is an overcorrection that fails to balance the identified opportunities with the constraints.
  141. The correct answer is C (A clear graphic overlay diagram highlighting key opportunities and constraints in plain language): An opportunities and constraints diagram translates technical site analysis into an accessible graphic format that clearly communicates key findings to stakeholders who may not have technical backgrounds. A raw geotechnical report, an unprocessed spreadsheet of boring data, or a verbal-only presentation without supporting graphics all fail to make the synthesized findings understandable to a general audience during a public planning process.
  142. The correct answer is A (It should inform circulation planning and potential phasing to establish access before development): A lack of existing access to an otherwise buildable area is a critical constraint that should directly inform circulation planning and phasing, since new roads or access easements may need to precede development in that area. Omitting the finding, disqualifying the whole site, or ignoring its bearing on land use decisions would waste a key insight the site analysis was specifically designed to surface.
  143. The correct answer is B (An opportunity to preserve and incorporate as a defining site amenity): A mature native tree stand is generally treated as an opportunity because it can be preserved and incorporated as a defining amenity, providing shade, habitat, and character that would take decades to replace if removed. It is not automatically a constraint requiring removal, an irrelevant feature, or an automatic disqualifying hazard, since its ecological and aesthetic value typically strengthens rather than limits the plan.
  144. The correct answer is D (As both a constraint on development location and an opportunity for a scenic overlook): Many site conditions carry dual significance, and a steep slope with strong views should be shown as both a constraint limiting where structures can be built and an opportunity for a scenic overlook or trail experience. Representing it as only a constraint or only an opportunity misses half of the relevant information, and omitting it from the diagram entirely would remove a condition that should directly shape the plan.
  145. The correct answer is C (To establish a shared, evidence-based foundation for generating and evaluating land use alternatives): The opportunities and constraints diagram translates site analysis into a clear foundation that the design team and stakeholders can use to generate and evaluate land use alternatives, ensuring subsequent decisions are grounded in documented site conditions. It is not itself the final adopted plan, it does not replace future public engagement, and it does not establish a construction cost, which comes later in the process.
  146. The correct answer is D (Locate compatible passive open space or trail uses within the easement area): Recognizing that a utility easement restricts structures but permits passive use allows the plan to productively locate trails, gardens, or open space within that area rather than leaving it unaddressed. Proposing a building within the easement ignores a legal restriction, while treating the land as entirely unusable wastes a viable opportunity. Assuming a no-cost relocation is an unreliable planning assumption without confirmation from the utility provider.
  147. The correct answers are A and D: A single condition, like a steep slope offering scenic views, frequently carries both constraint and opportunity value and should be represented as such, and the diagram itself should be built directly from the synthesized site analysis rather than assumptions. Constraints are not automatically slated for removal, since many, like floodplains or utility easements, are simply managed or worked around in the plan. A vision statement does not substitute for the detailed opportunities and constraints work needed to inform land use alternatives.
  148. The correct answer is A (Passive open space or recreational use that can tolerate periodic inundation): Passive open space and recreation are generally compatible with periodic flooding and can be designed to tolerate temporary inundation with minimal risk to life safety or costly infrastructure. Critical facilities like hospitals, permanent housing at the lowest grade, and unmitigated underground parking all place people and investment at elevated risk within an active floodplain and are typically discouraged or prohibited by regulation.
  149. The correct answer is B (Coordinate with the jurisdiction to align proposed land uses with both zoning and site constraints): Reconciling zoning with site constraints typically requires coordination with the jurisdiction, which may involve a variance, rezoning, or a land use that satisfies both the regulatory framework and the site's ecological limitations. Ignoring zoning outright or dismissing documented ecological constraints in its favor are both incomplete approaches. Proposing no land use avoids the conflict rather than resolving it through the available regulatory and design tools.
  150. The correct answer is C (Concentrating development on the upland area while preserving the wildlife corridor as connected open space): Concentrating development on stable, less sensitive land while keeping the wildlife corridor intact and connected preserves habitat function and reduces fragmentation, a widely recognized land use planning principle. Distributing development evenly, placing high-density uses directly within the corridor, or fragmenting it with scattered housing would all disrupt the corridor's ecological connectivity, the very function the plan should be protecting.
  151. The correct answer is A (The level of use or development a site can sustain without degrading its natural or social systems): Carrying capacity refers to the level of use or development that a site's natural and social systems can absorb without significant degradation, which is a distinct concept from a zoning ordinance's technical maximum, a project's budget, or a parking code requirement. Evaluating land use against carrying capacity ensures proposed uses remain sustainable over time rather than exceeding what the site's underlying resources can support.
  152. The correct answers are A and D: Directing higher-intensity uses toward stable land while protecting sensitive resources, and coordinating those decisions with applicable zoning and environmental regulations, reflects sound land use planning practice grounded in both ecological and legal considerations. Filling wetlands to simplify the process ignores regulatory protections and ecological function, and carrying capacity is directly relevant since it defines the level of use a site can sustainably support.
  153. The correct answer is B (How well each alternative achieves the established goals within identified opportunities and constraints): The preferred alternative should be the one that best achieves the project's established goals while respecting the documented opportunities and constraints, since that connects the final direction back to the evidence gathered earlier in the process. The amount of paved area, the order in which alternatives were drawn, or minimizing public review are not appropriate criteria for selecting a preferred master plan.
  154. The correct answer is D (A structured matrix comparing each alternative against the adopted goals and criteria): A structured evaluation matrix that compares each alternative against the same adopted goals and criteria gives stakeholders a transparent, consistent basis for decision-making. Presenting only one option, unlabeled renderings, or a description limited to the most expensive alternative all withhold the comparative information stakeholders need to meaningfully participate in selecting a preferred direction.
  155. The correct answer is D (Illustrative plans showing proposed circulation, open space, and land use organization): A master plan document typically includes illustrative plans that show the proposed organization of circulation, open space, and land use at a level of detail appropriate to guide future design phases. Final construction specifications, a signed contractor agreement, and detailed shop drawings belong to later design development and construction document phases, not to the master plan itself.
  156. The correct answer is A (Enough detail to establish organization, character, and feasibility without finalizing every design element): A master plan establishes overall organization, character, and feasibility, giving enough detail to guide subsequent schematic design and design development without prematurely locking in every element. Providing full construction-level detail skips necessary design phases, while offering no graphics or only a bare amenity list fails to communicate the spatial organization a master plan is meant to convey.
  157. The correct answer is B (Establishing clear implementation roles, partnerships, and funding strategies across jurisdictions): A multi-jurisdictional corridor plan depends on clearly identified implementation roles, partnership agreements, and funding strategies to move from adopted vision to built reality across different property owners and governments. Assuming a single owner will fund everything, avoiding coordination with adjacent jurisdictions, or leaving implementation responsibility unassigned all undermine the plan's ability to actually be carried out.
  158. The correct answers are A and D: A credible preferred alternative demonstrates a clear connection to the adopted vision and goals and is accompanied by realistic implementation strategies, including funding sources and a phasing approach, so decision-makers understand how the plan will actually be carried out. Selecting an alternative with no documented evaluation criteria undermines the transparency of the process, and an adopted master plan still requires schematic design and design development before construction can proceed.
  159. The correct answer is C (Scoring each scenario against a consistent set of weighted evaluation criteria): Applying a consistent, weighted set of evaluation criteria, such as cost, environmental performance, and goal achievement, to each scenario allows for an objective, transparent comparison. Personal aesthetic preference, order of completion, or a single stakeholder's opinion alone do not provide the systematic basis needed to evaluate competing planning scenarios fairly.
  160. The correct answer is A (Clearly document the trade-offs across criteria so decision-makers can make an informed choice): Transparently documenting how each alternative performs across all evaluation criteria, including where trade-offs exist, gives decision-makers the information needed to make a genuinely informed choice. Withholding unfavorable results, defaulting to the lowest-cost option, or silently merging scenarios without disclosure all obscure information that should be part of a transparent public decision-making process.
  161. The correct answer is B (It allows consistent, side-by-side comparison of alternatives across the same defined criteria): A scenario evaluation matrix structures the comparison so that each alternative is assessed against the same defined criteria, supporting a consistent and transparent side-by-side review. It does not guarantee unanimous stakeholder agreement, eliminate the need for further engagement, or remove the landscape architect's professional judgment, which still plays a role in interpreting and presenting the results.
  162. The correct answer is D (It reveals the true long-term financial and operational feasibility of each alternative): A life-cycle perspective accounts for both capital and ongoing operations and maintenance costs, revealing the true long-term financial and operational feasibility of each scenario rather than only its upfront price. It does not replace environmental or social evaluation criteria, does not automatically favor the lowest capital cost option, and is most useful before construction, when it can still influence which scenario is selected.
  163. The correct answer is C (To establish a long-range policy framework guiding future land use and development decisions): A land use plan establishes a long-range policy framework that guides future zoning amendments, capital investment, and development review decisions across a jurisdiction. It is not itself a construction contract, and it does not automatically replace the zoning ordinance, since implementing a land use plan typically requires separate legislative action to amend zoning. It also does not specify individual plant species at a citywide scale.
  164. The correct answer is A (An inventory of existing parks paired with an assessment of gaps in service and access): An effective parks and open space master plan inventories existing parks and assesses gaps in service, access, and equity across the municipality, so future investment can be prioritized where it is most needed. A single park rendering without citywide context, an unconnected plant list, or a mowing schedule alone do not provide the systemwide analysis needed to guide long-term capital investment decisions.
  165. The correct answer is D (A greenway plan emphasizes linear connectivity, multimodal access, and coordination across jurisdictions): Greenway plans are distinguished by their emphasis on linear connectivity, multimodal trail access, and the cross-jurisdictional coordination needed to link segments across multiple municipalities. They typically include significant ecological and habitat considerations rather than excluding them, they are not confined to a single municipal boundary, and they often require a land acquisition or easement strategy to assemble a continuous corridor.
  166. The correct answer is B (To identify significant historic resources and establish policies for their protection and treatment): A historic preservation plan identifies significant historic resources within the district and establishes policies and guidelines for their protection, treatment, and appropriate rehabilitation. It does not mandate demolition based on construction date, does not prohibit all new construction within the district, and does not substitute for Section 106 review, which remains a separate federal compliance process for federally involved undertakings.
  167. The correct answer is C (Flexibility to adapt to changing enrollment, funding, and programmatic needs over time): A twenty-year site master plan needs built-in flexibility so it can respond to shifting enrollment, funding availability, and programmatic priorities without becoming obsolete. A completely fixed design, a full set of construction documents for every future phase, or an assumption of unchanging enrollment would all undermine the plan's usefulness as conditions inevitably evolve over such a long horizon.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Engaging each agency early and aligning the plan with shared regional goals and priorities): Building consensus across multiple agencies requires early, ongoing engagement and aligning the plan with shared regional goals so each agency sees its own priorities reflected in the outcome. Finalizing the plan before informing agencies, assuming independent adoption without coordination, or excluding smaller agencies all reduce the likelihood of the broad, multi-agency buy-in a regional plan needs to succeed.
  2. The correct answers are B and C: A greenway plan is defined by its focus on linear connectivity and multimodal access that often crosses jurisdictional lines, while a parks and open space plan inventories what already exists and identifies where service gaps remain, guiding future investment. A regional plan by definition requires coordination among the jurisdictions it spans, and a land use plan is a policy document rather than a legally binding construction contract for a specific project.
  3. The correct answer is C (To establish a consistent framework of standards that future designers must follow): Design guidelines establish a consistent framework of standards, such as materials, scale, and character, that future designers and developers must follow to maintain coherence across a master plan area over time. They are not final construction drawings ready to bid, they typically support rather than eliminate ongoing design review, and they do not specify a particular contractor for future work.
  4. The correct answer is A (Standards for materials, plant palette, and streetscape elements applicable across the district): Effective design guidelines establish shared standards for materials, plant palette, and streetscape elements that apply consistently across the district, supporting visual coherence while allowing appropriate variation in building design. Requiring identical footprints regardless of use is overly rigid, prohibiting any future amendment ignores that guidelines often need periodic updates, and guidelines that expire after only the first phase would fail to guide the district over its full build-out.
  5. The correct answer is B (They provide a documented standard against which proposed modifications can be objectively evaluated): Design guidelines give a documented, objective standard that a reviewing body can apply consistently when evaluating proposed modifications, reducing arbitrary or inconsistent decision-making. They do not grant authority beyond what governing documents establish, they do not typically approve every proposal, and residential community guidelines commonly apply specifically to individual lots rather than excluding them.
  6. The correct answer is C (Establishing performance-based standards that define outcomes while allowing multiple design solutions): Performance-based guidelines define the desired outcome, such as scale, character, or stormwater performance, while allowing multiple design solutions to achieve it, balancing consistency with needed flexibility. Mandating a single fixed design for every application removes appropriate flexibility, providing no direction undermines consistency, and prohibiting all contemporary materials is an unnecessarily rigid restriction not required to achieve coherence.
  7. The correct answer is A (Sequencing phases to deliver early value while remaining financially and logistically feasible): A well-structured phasing plan sequences work so that each phase delivers meaningful, visible value to the community while remaining feasible given available funding and site logistics, helping sustain public and political support over time. Building the least visible portion first, attempting every phase simultaneously without adequate funding, or ignoring funding availability altogether all risk stalling or derailing implementation.
  8. The correct answer is D (Sequencing infrastructure improvements in an early phase that supports later amenity development): When infrastructure, such as utilities, grading, or access roads, must precede other work, the phasing plan should sequence it in an early phase that establishes the foundation later amenities depend on. Building amenities first without the needed infrastructure, ignoring the dependency, or assuming any sequence is workable risks costly rework or amenities that cannot properly function once built.
  9. The correct answer is B (A realistic sequence of phases tied to estimated costs, funding sources, and timeframes): A credible phasing plan for a long implementation horizon clearly ties each phase to realistic cost estimates, identified or likely funding sources, and general timeframes, giving decision-makers confidence the plan is achievable. A single completion date without detail, reliance on one unconfirmed grant, or a plan that addresses only the first year fail to demonstrate the long-term feasibility a governing board needs to see.
  10. The correct answer is A (Early open space can establish community character and increase the marketability of later phases): Delivering open space early can establish a desirable community character and amenity base that increases the marketability and value of later development phases, a common strategy in master planned communities. It is not accurate that open space has little bearing on marketability, that early open space is a universal jurisdictional requirement, or that open space should typically be reserved for the final phase, since sequencing depends on project-specific goals.
  11. The correct answers are A and B: Sound phasing sequences dependent infrastructure ahead of the amenities that rely on it and grounds the schedule in realistic, identified funding availability rather than assumptions. Requiring every phase to be built simultaneously ignores real-world capital constraints, and a well-sequenced phasing plan actually has significant bearing on community perception, since early, visible progress helps sustain public support for the remaining phases.
  12. The correct answer is C (A concise summary connecting the plan's recommendations back to the original goals and public input): An effective presentation to elected officials concisely connects the plan's recommendations back to the original goals and the public input that shaped them, helping decision-makers understand the plan's rationale. A lengthy technical document without a summary, a presentation limited only to cost figures, or one that omits the engagement process fails to give officials the context they need to make an informed adoption decision.
  13. The correct answer is D (Providing both a detailed technical document and an accessible, graphic-forward summary): Serving both technical reviewers and the general public well typically requires a detailed technical document to support professional review alongside an accessible, graphic-forward summary that communicates key outcomes to a broader audience. Producing only one of these formats, or assuming a single document will serve every audience equally, fails to meet the different needs of these distinct groups.
  14. The correct answer is B (Using clear, consistent legends, labels, and scale references across all plan graphics): Clear, consistent legends, labels, and scale references across all plan graphics allow viewers, both technical and general audiences, to accurately interpret what is being proposed. Omitting a legend, varying symbol conventions from sheet to sheet, or leaving scale unspecified all create ambiguity that undermines the public's ability to understand the plan's actual recommendations.
  15. The correct answer is D (It provides a lasting, accessible reference that guides consistent implementation over time): A well-prepared summary document gives agency staff and future project teams an accessible, lasting reference that supports consistent implementation of the adopted plan's intent over time. It does not replace the need to retain the full detailed plan, it is not limited to use only at the adoption meeting, and future design teams still benefit from reviewing the adopted plan directly as projects move forward.
  16. The correct answer is B (Establishing periodic progress reporting tied to measurable implementation milestones): Establishing periodic reporting tied to measurable milestones, such as completed phases or acquired funding, keeps stakeholders informed and maintains accountability throughout a long implementation period. Reporting only once at adoption, avoiding disclosure of challenges, or limiting communication to internal staff alone all undermine the transparency needed to sustain public trust and support over the life of the plan.
  17. The correct answers are A and C: Consistent graphic conventions across the document set and an accessible summary paired with the technical plan both support clear understanding across varied audiences, from technical reviewers to the general public. Assuming communication materials never need updating ignores that plans evolve during implementation, and ongoing progress reporting remains important well after adoption to maintain transparency and public trust throughout the implementation period.
  18. The correct answer is A (The client needs third-party verification of performance to satisfy funders or regulatory incentives): Formal certification provides independent, third-party verification of sustainable performance, which is often required or incentivized by funders, grant programs, or local regulations. Avoiding documentation, a small site size, or a lack of interest in demonstrating performance are reasons a client might forgo certification rather than reasons to pursue it, since certification inherently requires documentation regardless of site size.
  19. The correct answer is C (Healthy soil supports plant establishment, stormwater infiltration, and long-term carbon storage): Restored, healthy soil supports robust plant establishment, allows water to infiltrate rather than run off, and can sequester carbon over time, making it a foundational strategy that supports multiple sustainability goals at once. It does not eliminate the need for irrigation or erosion control entirely, and it has a well-documented, measurable effect on stormwater performance rather than none at all.
  20. The correct answer is D (Specifying regionally native and climate-adapted species suited to existing site conditions): Regionally native and climate-adapted species are suited to local rainfall and soil conditions, reducing long-term irrigation demand while supporting the pollinators and wildlife adapted to those same species. A uniform imported ornamental palette, extensive turfgrass, or species that require continuous irrigation all work against both the water conservation and biodiversity goals the plan is meant to achieve.
  21. The correct answer is C (Distributing shaded cooling refuges and tree canopy throughout publicly accessible open space): Distributing shaded cooling refuges and expanding tree canopy across publicly accessible space directly reduces heat exposure risk for residents during extreme heat events, particularly for populations without reliable access to air conditioning. Removing shade trees increases heat exposure, concentrating new planting on one private parcel does not serve the broader public, and assuming existing infrastructure is sufficient ignores the documented trend of increasing heat events.
  22. The correct answer is A (Establishing defensible space and fuel breaks between wildland vegetation and structures): Defensible space and fuel breaks create separation between wildland vegetation and structures, slowing fire spread and giving firefighters a more defensible perimeter to protect buildings. Planting dense vegetation directly against foundations increases risk, ignoring vegetation management contradicts established wildfire mitigation practice, and concentrating development within the highest hazard zones increases exposure rather than reducing it.
  23. The correct answer is B (Designing landscapes with capacity to both infiltrate stormwater and tolerate periods of drought): Landscapes designed with both infiltration capacity for intense storms and drought tolerance for dry periods are resilient across the full range of conditions a changing climate can produce. Designing for only one narrow condition, relying solely on potable irrigation, or removing vegetation altogether all fail to provide the flexible performance a resilient regional plan requires.
  24. The correct answer is C (Mapping park access alongside demographic and income data to identify underserved areas): Mapping park access together with demographic and income data reveals which neighborhoods are underserved relative to their population, supporting a targeted, equity-based investment strategy. Mapping only recent parks, assuming equity without analysis, or prioritizing the highest-value neighborhoods all fail to identify or address the disparities an equity-focused parks master plan is meant to correct.
  25. The correct answer is A (Providing continuous, gently graded routes and seating options integrated throughout the plaza): Universal design integrates continuous, gently graded routes and seating throughout the space so all visitors can move and gather together, rather than relying on separate or segregated accommodations. A single rear accessible entrance, a requirement to request assistance, or isolated accessible seating all create separate and unequal experiences rather than one universally usable environment.
  26. The correct answer is D (Whether the plaza retains sufficient integrity and historic or design significance to convey its period): Eligibility for a cultural landscape inventory depends on whether the resource retains enough physical integrity and historic or design significance to convey its important period, not simply on current popularity or promotional appearances. A plaza designed within the last five years would not yet meet the age and historic significance thresholds typically applied to such evaluations.
  27. The correct answer is B (Engaging descendant communities and documenting the site's significance before finalizing any plan): Appropriate practice engages descendant communities and thoroughly documents the site's historic and cultural significance before finalizing any master plan decisions affecting it. Removing physical evidence, proceeding without further research, or treating the site as an ordinary undeveloped parcel all fail to recognize the profound cultural and historic significance such a resource typically holds.
  28. The correct answers are B and D: Section 106 requires identifying historic properties within the area a federal undertaking could affect and consulting with the State Historic Preservation Officer, and often other stakeholders, to resolve any adverse effects before the project proceeds. It does not automatically or permanently halt federally funded work, since the process is designed to reach a resolution through consultation, and it specifically applies to undertakings with federal funding, licensing, or permitting involvement rather than purely private projects.
  29. The correct answer is C (Specific, measurable language that allows future progress to be assessed): Goals written in specific, measurable language allow the planning team and stakeholders to later assess whether the plan is achieving its intended outcomes, supporting accountability during implementation. Vague language open to multiple interpretations, a single undescribed word, or generic language copied from an unrelated project all fail to provide the clarity needed to evaluate progress over time.
  30. The correct answer is B (Grounding goals in both stakeholder input and findings from the site analysis): Well-grounded goals synthesize both community priorities gathered through engagement and the technical realities documented through site analysis, so the resulting direction is both desired and achievable. Relying exclusively on stakeholder input while ignoring site conditions, or the reverse, produces goals that are either technically unrealistic or disconnected from community priorities. Borrowing generic goals from another jurisdiction ignores local context entirely.
  31. The correct answer is D (Present the client with cost implications and realistic alternatives before proceeding further): A professionally appropriate response clearly presents the cost implications of the request alongside realistic alternatives, allowing the client to make an informed decision before design proceeds further. Designing the amenity anyway and dealing with the shortfall later, refusing further discussion, or assuming unconfirmed funding will appear all create avoidable risk and erode the client's trust in the planning process.
  32. The correct answer is A (Facilitating a structured prioritization process that weighs each group's needs against site capacity and budget): A structured prioritization process that weighs competing needs against site capacity, program compatibility, and budget produces a program that reflects the community's priorities within real constraints. Granting every request without regard to capacity or funding, favoring only the first submission, or excluding user input altogether all fail to produce a realistic, well-balanced program.
  33. The correct answer is D (To provide a general cost range that supports early decision-making before detailed design exists): An order-of-magnitude estimate provides a general cost range based on comparable projects and program scope, supporting early decisions about feasibility and priorities before detailed design work exists. It is not a final guaranteed price, it does not replace the more detailed estimates prepared during design development, and it does not calculate specific subcontractor wage rates.
  34. The correct answer is A (Document the conflict clearly and explore alternatives that balance both values): Effective synthesis clearly documents conflicting findings, such as competing solar access and soil quality, and explores alternatives that thoughtfully balance both values rather than defaulting to one without analysis. Automatically favoring either finding without deliberate evaluation, or omitting one entirely, undermines the purpose of a comprehensive site analysis synthesis.
  35. The correct answer is B (Where the formal circulation system may not be meeting actual user movement patterns): Comparing desire lines, the informal paths people actually walk, against the formal circulation network reveals where the designed system fails to serve how people actually move through a site, informing future circulation planning. This comparison does not determine construction costs, building square footage, or a planting palette, which depend on separate programming and design decisions.
  36. The correct answer is C (Relative appropriateness of different site areas for various land uses based on combined criteria): A suitability map combines multiple analysis criteria, such as slope, soils, and hydrology, into a composite view showing the relative appropriateness of different areas for various potential land uses. It is a planning tool that informs, rather than finalizes, land use boundaries, and it does not specify construction materials or serve as a legal property description.
  37. The correct answers are B and D: Sound synthesis practice documents conflicting findings transparently and explores alternatives that thoughtfully balance competing values, such as agricultural soil quality against solar access, rather than resolving the tension silently or arbitrarily. Automatically excluding high-quality agricultural soil from further analysis is an overcorrection that skips deliberate evaluation, and synthesis is precisely what should inform and shape the land use alternatives developed later in the process.
  38. The correct answer is C (By encouraging building height and placement that preserve key sightlines to the viewshed): A valued scenic viewshed identified during opportunities and constraints analysis should inform building height, massing, and placement so that key sightlines are preserved as the site develops. Maximizing building height everywhere, ignoring the finding altogether, or removing all vegetation that might frame rather than block the view would all undermine the resource the analysis identified as worth protecting.
  39. The correct answer is B (Considering buffer plantings, setbacks, or compatible land uses to mitigate noise impacts): Addressing an adjacent noise source with buffer plantings, increased setbacks, or land uses that are naturally more tolerant of noise reflects a responsive constraint analysis. Ignoring the condition because it originates offsite, placing the most noise-sensitive use directly against the source, or assuming users will not notice the noise all fail to adequately respond to a documented site constraint.
  40. The correct answer is D (Directing new development away from the prime soils and toward already disturbed portions of the site): Directing new development toward already disturbed land while protecting prime agricultural soils balances growth pressure with the long-term value of a limited and largely irreplaceable resource. Concentrating dense development on the best soils, ignoring soil quality in land use decisions, or assuming agricultural soils simply regenerate on their own all disregard well-established land use planning principles for protecting agricultural resources.
  41. The correct answer is A (Limiting land use to low-impact, compatible activities that preserve habitat connectivity): Limiting land use within a wildlife corridor to low-impact, compatible activities preserves the connectivity that gives the corridor its ecological function. Proposing high-density development, disregarding a previously documented designation, or fragmenting the corridor with new roads would all compromise the habitat connectivity the land use decision should be protecting.
  42. The correct answers are C and D: Sound land use practice directs new development toward already disturbed portions of a site to protect prime agricultural soils, and it preserves habitat connectivity within a wildlife corridor by limiting that area to compatible, low-impact uses. Disregarding an earlier corridor designation ignores documented ecological analysis, and dismissing agricultural soil quality as irrelevant contradicts widely accepted land use planning principles for protecting limited natural resources.
  43. The correct answer is D (Revisiting circulation and land use assumptions to address the documented traffic concerns): Responsive master planning revisits the relevant circulation and land use assumptions when specific, documented concerns like traffic impacts are raised, refining the plan accordingly. Dismissing the feedback because the plan was already presented once, ignoring it due to scope limitations, or removing all roadways without further analysis all fail to genuinely address the concern stakeholders raised.
  44. The correct answer is B (General building footprints, height, and massing parameters that guide but do not finalize architecture): A master plan typically establishes general building footprints, height limits, and massing parameters that provide guidance for future architectural design without finalizing it. Fully detailed elevations and floor plans belong to later architectural design phases, omitting any building guidance leaves future development undirected, and requiring identical prefabricated structures is an unnecessarily rigid constraint not typical of master plan guidance.
  45. The correct answer is A (Balancing traffic capacity, pedestrian safety, and connections to surrounding land uses): Determining primary access requires balancing vehicular traffic capacity, pedestrian and bicycle safety, and meaningful connections to surrounding land uses and transit, since access decisions shape how the entire site functions. Choosing based on minimal signage needs, deliberately avoiding transit connections, or assuming all entry points perform equally are not appropriate bases for this important master planning decision.
  46. The correct answer is C (The adopted vision, goals, and objectives established during the planning process): Evaluation criteria should be directly derived from the adopted vision, goals, and objectives so that scenario evaluation remains consistent with what the planning process set out to achieve. Basing criteria on personal preference, choosing whichever scenario requires the least analysis, or borrowing unrelated criteria from another project undermines the connection between the plan's stated priorities and how alternatives are ultimately judged.
  47. The correct answer is B (Matching plant selection to the specific microclimate and rooting conditions of the planting location): Matching plant selection to the specific microclimate and rooting conditions of the parking structure location ensures species will actually survive reflected heat and limited soil volume. Selecting the cheapest material without accounting for conditions, reusing a shaded-area palette in a hot exposed location, or ignoring microclimate largely would likely lead to plant failure.
  138. The correct answer is D (Specifying paving with a higher solar reflectance index and increasing canopy shade coverage): Specifying paving with a higher solar reflectance index while increasing canopy shade coverage directly reduces heat island effect in a hot climate. Dark paving with no shade, eliminating all planting, or ignoring solar reflectance's effect on comfort would all worsen rather than improve thermal comfort.
  139. The correct answer is A (Providing adequately sized and located access points for anticipated maintenance equipment): Providing adequately sized and located access points for anticipated maintenance equipment ensures the planted median can actually be maintained over time. Assuming staff will cross traffic lanes on foot, omitting access entirely, or sizing access for pedestrians only would each create real long-term maintenance and safety problems.
  140. The correct answers are A and B: Grouping plants with similar water and light requirements together, and selecting mature sizes and spacing appropriate to the intended long-term coverage, both reduce future maintenance burden while supporting healthy growth. Maximizing distinct species regardless of complexity or specifying plants without regard to soil compatibility would each increase maintenance demands instead.
  141. The correct answer is C (Material durability and ease of repair under anticipated public use conditions): Material durability and ease of repair under anticipated public use conditions are the most relevant considerations for a heavily used, vandalism-prone fountain finish. Matching an unrelated interior scheme, choosing the most costly maintenance option, or ignoring durability entirely fail to address the public-use context described.
  142. The correct answer is A (Selecting a durable, well-draining surface material suited to heavy use and low maintenance capacity): Selecting a durable, well-draining surface suited to heavy use and the owner's limited maintenance capacity directly responds to both the dog park's demands and the stated staffing constraint. A delicate groundcover, ignoring the owner's maintenance capacity, or specifying a labor-intensive surface would all conflict with the described conditions.
  143. The correct answer is B (Review the utility survey first, then adjust tree pit locations to maintain required clearances): Reviewing the utility survey first, then adjusting tree pit locations to maintain required clearances, follows the correct sequence for avoiding conflicts with existing underground infrastructure. Finalizing tree pit locations before requesting the survey, ignoring the survey, or assuming the utility company will relocate its own infrastructure all risk real conflicts.
  144. The correct answer is D (Species with demonstrated tolerance for the fluctuating moisture conditions typical of rain gardens): Species with demonstrated tolerance for the fluctuating moisture conditions typical of rain gardens are essential, since the planting must handle both saturated and dry periods depending on runoff. Species requiring consistently dry conditions, selection with no reference to hydrology, or selection based only on nursery availability would each risk plant failure.
  145. The correct answer is C (Testing bench orientation against seasonal sun angles and desired social interaction patterns): Testing bench orientation against seasonal sun angles and desired social interaction patterns ensures seating is both comfortable and functionally supportive of how people will actually use the space. Orienting all benches identically, ignoring sun exposure, or basing orientation on shipping constraints ignore the functional and comfort goals of the refinement.
  146. The correct answer is D (Select species tolerant of compacted, poorly drained conditions or plan for soil amendment strategies): Selecting species tolerant of compacted, poorly drained conditions, or planning for soil amendment, directly responds to the clay soil identified on the site. Species requiring well-drained sandy soil, ignoring soil conditions largely, or selecting based only on availability would likely lead to poor tree performance or failure.
  147. The correct answers are A, B, and C: Conserving irrigation water depends on grouping plants by hydrozone, selecting a controller that can adjust for weather or soil moisture data, and matching application rates to the site's actual soil infiltration characteristics. Applying one uniform schedule regardless of differing plant water needs works directly against water conservation goals.
  148. The correct answer is B (Selecting drought-tolerant, climate-appropriate plants and minimizing high-water-use turf areas): Selecting drought-tolerant, climate-appropriate plants while minimizing high-water-use turf area is the most effective strategy for reducing potable water use in a xeriscape garden. Increasing turf area, relying solely on a smart controller with no plant selection strategy, or specifying tropical species without close attention to climate would all work against the water conservation goal.
  149. The correct answer is C (To group plants into hydrozones so irrigation can be applied efficiently according to actual demand): Classifying planting areas by water use groups plants into hydrozones so irrigation can be applied efficiently according to actual demand, which is the core purpose of this collaboration. It does not identify plants needing zero irrigation under all circumstances, does not end further consultant collaboration, and is not based on retail cost.
  150. The correct answer is D (A graywater or reclaimed water irrigation system with appropriate treatment and application methods): A system using treated wastewater from an on-site source for subsurface irrigation of ornamental beds is a graywater or reclaimed water system requiring appropriate treatment and application methods. A standard potable spray system, an untreated potable distribution system, and an unrelated stormwater detention basin do not match the described treated on-site source.
  151. The correct answer is D (Whether the proposed planting and irrigation design complies with applicable water conservation limits): Comparing the estimated landscape water use to the jurisdiction's water budget requirement primarily determines whether the proposed planting and irrigation design complies with applicable conservation limits. It does not evaluate the overall construction budget, the contractor's crew size, or the need for structural engineering review.
  152. The correct answer is A (Reduced water loss to evaporation, runoff, and overspray onto hardscape surfaces): Drip emitters reduce water loss to evaporation, runoff, and overspray onto adjacent hardscape compared with overhead spray, which is their primary benefit near a walkway. Drip systems do not eliminate all future maintenance, do not intentionally apply water onto paved surfaces, and do not remove the need for hydrozone grouping.
  153. The correct answer is C (Reviewing microclimate, sun exposure, and soil conditions to identify appropriate low water use zones): Reviewing microclimate, sun exposure, and soil conditions is the most effective first step for identifying which parts of the site are genuinely suitable for low water use planting. Randomly assigning zones, applying low water use planting uniformly regardless of conditions, or deferring the water budget analysis until later would each undermine an effective, site-responsive plan.
  154. The correct answers are A and B: A graywater irrigation system must account for applicable health and safety regulations governing its use, and it typically requires coordination between the landscape architect and a plumbing or irrigation consultant with the relevant expertise. Graywater cannot always be applied through unrestricted overhead spray, and its use does not eliminate the need for hydrozoning or plant water requirement analysis.
  155. The correct answer is B (The site's hydrozone complexity, size, and the owner's capacity to manage the system): The site's hydrozone complexity, size, and the owner's capacity to manage the system are the primary factors that should drive the choice between a centralized and decentralized irrigation controller. Housing color, use on an unrelated project with a different climate, or minimal documentation requirements are not meaningful factors in this technical decision.
  156. The correct answer is C (Preventing unnecessary irrigation when natural rainfall has already met plant water needs): A rain sensor's primary water conservation benefit is preventing unnecessary irrigation cycles when natural rainfall has already met the plants' water needs. It does not guarantee irrigation will never run again for the season, does not remove the need for hydrozone classification, and does not increase water use after rain events.
  157. The correct answer is A (Explaining that well-designed low water use planting can achieve strong visual character while reducing water demand): Explaining that well-designed low water use planting can achieve strong visual character while reducing water demand accurately addresses the client's aesthetic concern with a factual response. Claiming such gardens typically resemble bare gravel, that conservation and visual quality are mutually exclusive, or recommending abandoning conservation largely misrepresent what thoughtful low water use design can achieve.
  158. The correct answer is D (Turf and shrub plantings typically have different water application rates and scheduling needs): Turf and shrub or groundcover plantings typically have different water application rates and scheduling needs, which is the primary reason for separating them onto different irrigation circuits. Circuit separation does not eliminate the need for hydrozone analysis, and established turf still requires irrigation, unlike the claim that it does not.
  159. The correct answer is B (Specifying cycle and soak irrigation scheduling or check valves to allow water absorption between cycles): Specifying cycle and soak scheduling or check valves allows water to be absorbed between shorter irrigation cycles, reducing runoff on a sloped planting bed. Increasing the application rate would worsen runoff, removing all planting is an extreme and unnecessary response, and ignoring the slope disregards a real design constraint.
  160. The correct answer is C (The volume, quality, and seasonal availability of captured runoff relative to the planting's water demand): Evaluating the volume, quality, and seasonal availability of captured roof runoff relative to the planting's actual water demand determines whether it can meaningfully supplement irrigation. Cistern appearance, roof color matching, or applying runoff without regard to water quality do not address whether the supply is sufficient or safe to use.
  161. The correct answers are A and B: Soil infiltration rate affects how quickly irrigation water can be absorbed without generating runoff, and amending compacted or sandy soils can improve water retention and reduce overall irrigation demand. Soil conditions have real bearing on both scheduling and plant selection, and different soil textures do not absorb or retain water identically.
  162. The correct answer is D (Identify the board's specific submission requirements and timeline before finalizing the design development package): Identifying the design review board's specific submission requirements and timeline before finalizing the design development package allows the schedule to properly accommodate the required approval process. Submitting directly for a permit, assuming automatic approval, or waiting until construction is complete would all bypass a required review step.
  163. The correct answer is A (Identify the variance requirement early and factor the approval process into the project schedule): Identifying the variance requirement early and factoring the approval process into the project schedule accounts for the additional time a zoning variance typically requires. Proceeding to construction documents without addressing the conflict, assuming automatic approval, or hiding the issue from the client all create schedule and compliance risk.
  164. The correct answer is B (Research the applicable regulatory requirements and initiate coordination with the agency early in design): Researching the applicable regulatory requirements and initiating agency coordination early in design allows the project to account for a wetland-related review before it affects the schedule. Waiting until construction, assuming no approval is needed simply because work occurs outside the wetland boundary, or proceeding to final documents first all risk regulatory noncompliance.
  165. The correct answer is C (The project schedule and design development sequencing to accommodate the required review process): A required stormwater authority review should influence the project schedule and design development sequencing so the review can be accommodated without delaying construction. Claiming the review has only limited bearing on design development, applies only at the construction document stage, or should lead to largely eliminating stormwater management all misstate its actual influence.
  166. The correct answer is D (Confirm the commission's review criteria and submission process before finalizing design details): Confirming the architectural review commission's criteria and submission process before finalizing design details ensures the landscape design meets the historic district's requirements. Assuming the commission's authority is limited to building facades, ignoring its review, or assuming its jurisdiction excludes new construction all risk submitting noncompliant designs.
  167. The correct answer is B (Identify the health department's specific requirements and incorporate them into the design development process): Identifying the health department's specific requirements for a recirculating water feature and incorporating them into the design development process addresses the permitting need proactively. Assuming a decorative fountain needs no permitting, waiting until after construction, or removing the fountain without research all fail to properly investigate an actual regulatory requirement.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answers are A and B: Identifying applicable approval requirements early helps the project team plan an accurate schedule, and coordinating with the client and relevant agencies is a normal part of managing the design process effectively. Required approvals are not solely the contractor's responsibility during design phases, and they can meaningfully affect decisions made during both schematic design and design development.
  2. The correct answer is C (Coordinate design details with the department's design standards and submission process): Coordinating design details with the state department of transportation's design standards and submission process is necessary because the streetscape lies along a state-maintained roadway. Ignoring the department's standards, assuming automatic approval, or waiting until construction documents are complete before contacting the department all create real schedule and approval risk.
  3. The correct answer is A (Research applicable floodplain requirements and incorporate necessary design accommodations early): Researching applicable floodplain requirements and incorporating necessary design accommodations early addresses the regulated crossing before it affects later design phases. Designing without consideration of the floodplain, assuming regulations apply only to buildings, or deferring consideration until after construction begins all risk noncompliance with the ordinance.
  4. The correct answer is D (Incorporate the ordinance's requirements into the design and initiate the required permitting process): Incorporating the tree preservation ordinance's requirements into the design and initiating the required permitting process is the appropriate response once the ordinance is identified. Ignoring the ordinance, removing the protected trees to avoid it, or assuming the contractor will handle compliance without design input all risk noncompliance and loss of valuable trees.
  5. The correct answer is A (Research the coastal zone requirements and factor the review timeline into the project schedule): Researching the coastal zone requirements and factoring the review timeline into the project schedule accounts for a permit process that can meaningfully affect grading design decisions. Proceeding without research, assuming permits apply only to structures, or waiting until after construction to check would all create real regulatory risk.
  6. The correct answer is C (Early identification allows the schedule, budget, and design to account for review timelines and requirements): Early identification of required approvals lets the schedule, budget, and design account for review timelines and requirements before they become obstacles later in the project. Claiming approvals guarantee no future design revisions, have no bearing on schematic design, or matter only after substantial completion all misstate the actual benefit of early identification.
  7. The correct answer is B (Coordinate submissions with both reviewing entities and account for their requirements in the design schedule): Coordinating submissions with both the municipal engineer and the regional water authority, and accounting for their requirements in the schedule, addresses the fact that both entities must review the detention facility. Submitting to only one agency, ignoring either agency's technical requirements, or waiting until construction documents to determine jurisdiction all risk delay or rejection.
  8. The correct answers are A, B, and C: Depending on project location and scope, a landscape architect may reasonably need to coordinate with a local design or architectural review board, obtain a zoning variance or conditional use approval for a nonconforming element, or secure stormwater or environmental agency review for regulated drainage improvements. An informal social media poll has no standing as a project approval process.
  9. The correct answer is D (Compile a checklist of required approvals and their status to inform the project schedule going forward): Compiling a checklist of required approvals and their current status gives the project team an accurate basis for planning the remaining schedule before finalizing construction documents. Assuming all approvals were typically completed, proceeding without confirming status, or delegating tracking largely to the client all risk missing a required approval.
  10. The correct answer is A (Opinion of probable construction cost): A preliminary construction cost figure developed during schematic design using unit costs and approximate quantities is properly called an opinion of probable construction cost. A guaranteed maximum price and a stipulated sum bid are contractor pricing mechanisms, and a certificate for payment relates to construction administration payment approval, none of which apply at this stage.
  11. The correct answer is C (An opinion of probable cost is an estimate based on available information, while a bid reflects a specific contractor's actual pricing and market conditions): An opinion of probable cost is an estimate based on the information available at the time, while an actual bid reflects a specific contractor's pricing and current market conditions, which explains why the two figures can differ. The landscape architect does not guarantee the final construction cost, the two figures are not required to match by law, and a difference does not automatically indicate a drafting error.
  12. The correct answer is D (More refined quantities and specific material selections available at the design development stage): More refined quantities and specific material selections available at the design development stage allow the updated opinion of probable cost to be more accurate than the earlier schematic-level estimate. Guaranteeing the final bid, removing contingency to lower the figure, or relying on a single unrelated project would each undermine rather than improve estimate accuracy.
  13. The correct answer is B (The level of skill and care ordinarily exercised by other reasonably prudent landscape architects under similar circumstances): Standard of care refers to the level of skill and care ordinarily exercised by other reasonably prudent landscape architects under similar circumstances, not a guarantee of error-free work. It is not an obligation limited only to licensed engineers, and it does not promise that the final construction cost will never exceed the original opinion of probable cost.
  14. The correct answers are A and B: Errors and omissions insurance provides financial protection related to claims alleging professional negligence in delivering services, and it is a distinct form of coverage from general liability insurance, which addresses different categories of risk. It does not guarantee that a landscape architect can never be found liable, and it does not eliminate the professional obligation to meet the applicable standard of care.
  15. The correct answer is C (Statutes of limitations and repose, which generally establish time limits for bringing legal claims against design professionals): Statutes of limitations and repose generally establish time limits for bringing legal claims against design professionals, which is the relevant general concept for long-term liability exposure. There is no guarantee that a claim can rarely be filed, claims are not required to be filed before construction begins, and liability exposure does not end immediately upon submitting the design development package.
  16. The correct answer is A (Whether the substitution was evaluated and documented consistent with the applicable standard of care): Whether a value-engineered substitution was evaluated and documented consistent with the applicable standard of care is the key factor in assessing later liability exposure. Choosing based solely on lowest cost, relying on undocumented verbal approval, or personally assuring the client the substitution carried no future risk do not establish that the applicable standard of care was met.
  17. The correct answer is C (It may increase liability exposure beyond what standard errors and omissions coverage is designed to address): Contract language attempting to hold the firm to a higher-than-ordinary standard of care may increase liability exposure beyond what standard errors and omissions coverage is designed to address. Such language does have a real effect on liability exposure, does not automatically void a professional license, and does not guarantee the firm will never be sued.
  18. The correct answer is A (It creates a record demonstrating that decisions were made consistent with the applicable standard of care): Documenting design decisions, client approvals, and the basis for substitutions creates a record demonstrating that decisions were made consistent with the applicable standard of care. This documentation does not guarantee an absence of construction defects, does not eliminate the need for errors and omissions insurance, and does not transfer professional liability to the client.
  19. The correct answers are A, B, and C: Managing professional liability risk generally involves maintaining clear written documentation of design decisions and client approvals, evaluating decisions against the applicable standard of care, and carrying appropriate errors and omissions insurance coverage. Avoiding written communication with the client to reduce discoverable evidence is poor practice that undermines, rather than supports, sound risk management.
  20. The correct answer is B (It establishes horizontal control, dimensions, and geometry for hardscape and site elements): a grading plan communicates vertical elevations and drainage, but it does not typically carry the precise dimensioning contractors need to stake hardscape. A dedicated layout plan provides coordinates, radii, and offsets. Together the two sheets give the contractor complete horizontal and vertical control.
  21. The correct answer is D (Cover sheet, general notes, and existing conditions or demolition plan): construction document sets are conventionally organized so a reviewer encounters project overview information first, followed by baseline site conditions before design sheets. This sequence supports logical cross-referencing during bidding and construction. Placing punch lists or closeout items first would be inconsistent with standard drawing conventions.
  22. The correct answer is C (Drawing legibility and scale needed to clearly convey each scope without clutter): when multiple scopes are combined on one sheet the drawing can become too dense to read clearly, especially at a small scale. Separating scopes preserves clarity for bidders and field crews. The decision should be driven by communication needs, not printer capability or unrelated preferences.
  23. The correct answer is B (Setting Bed): the setting bed is the thin layer of sand or aggregate screeded to an exact thickness that gives unit pavers a level, stable surface to rest on. Its material, gradation, and thickness must be called out precisely on a materials and layout plan, since a planting plan is not intended to carry hardscape construction detail. Without this callout, the contractor lacks the dimensional control needed to install a flat, properly draining paved surface. Omitting it risks an uneven or unbuildable paving assembly.
  24. The correct answers are A and C: a dimensioned layout plan with coordinate control and construction details keyed to sections are construction document deliverables meant to direct actual building. Conceptual bubble diagrams and loose massing sketches are design development or earlier phase tools used to establish intent, not to direct construction. Construction documents must be specific and buildable.
  25. The correct answer is D (The demolition plan shows removals while the protection plan identifies elements to be preserved and safeguarded): these sheets serve distinct but complementary purposes on a renovation project. The demolition plan directs what comes out, while the protection plan directs what must be shielded from construction activity. Both are necessary to avoid unintended damage to retained features.
  26. The correct answer is C (Existing utilities, structures, vegetation, and topography verified against the survey): an accurate existing conditions plan is the foundation for all subsequent design and construction decisions. Design elements like planting palette or furniture selections belong to later design phases, not the existing conditions base. Cost estimates are a separate deliverable entirely.
  27. The correct answer is B (Verify the conflict in the field and issue a clarification or revised demolition plan): conflicting information between consultants must be resolved through verification rather than assumption. Removing both elements or ignoring the arborist's findings could cause unnecessary loss of a tree intended for preservation. A timely field verification and written clarification prevents costly errors.
  28. The correct answers are A, B, and C: a demolition plan should identify items for removal, relocation, or protection, disturbance and staging limits, and utilities to be abandoned or relocated. A final plant species list belongs to the planting plan, not the demolition plan, since demolition documentation focuses on removals and site preparation rather than new design content.
  29. The correct answer is A (Fencing at the trunk fails to protect the root zone from compaction and construction damage): tree protection fencing must extend to the critical root zone, which is generally much wider than the trunk itself, to prevent soil compaction and root severance. Fencing at the trunk line offers essentially no meaningful protection and should be corrected before construction begins.
  30. The correct answer is C (Coordinate a root protection zone and note grading restrictions on the protection plan): grading operations near a tree's root zone can cause significant, sometimes fatal, root damage. The landscape architect should document restricted grading limits and coordinate with the civil engineer so cut and fill activity respects the tree's critical root zone.
  31. The correct answer is D (It documents required replacement plantings and monitoring measures to offset unavoidable impacts): mitigation plans are regulatory documents intended to demonstrate that unavoidable environmental disturbance will be offset through replacement planting and monitoring. They are not optional exhibits, and they do not substitute for erosion control documentation elsewhere in the set.
  32. The correct answers are A and B: fence type, location, and signage, along with the critical root zone radius for each protected tree, are essential elements of a tree protection plan. Hardscape paving patterns and irrigation wiring diagrams belong on other sheets and are unrelated to the specific purpose of protecting existing trees during construction.
  33. The correct answer is B (Errors in either building corner compound and make field layout less reliable): dimensioning from two independent reference points allows small survey or as-built discrepancies in either point to compound into layout error. A single reliable baseline or benchmark generally produces more accurate and repeatable field staking than triangulating from two separate references.
  34. The correct answer is B (Concrete Footing): the footing is the structural element that transfers the wall's loads to the subgrade and sets the fixed bearing elevation and plan geometry the rest of the wall is built from. Because it is curved, its radius, arc length, and coordinate points must be tied to a baseline or benchmark so the surveyor can stake it accurately. An error in footing location or elevation propagates upward through the CMU wall and cap. A single overall end-to-end dimension cannot convey this curved geometry.
  35. The correct answer is D (Coordinate with the utility owner and civil engineer to confirm the line's exact location and depth): an unresolved utility conflict beneath a proposed plaza creates significant risk during construction. Verifying the exact location and depth before finalizing the layout allows the design to be adjusted or the utility relocated in a coordinated, documented manner.
  36. The correct answer is A (Clear disposal requirements avoid bid ambiguity and support compliance with local waste regulations): without documented disposal or recycling expectations, bidders may price the work inconsistently and the project may fail to meet local regulatory requirements. Adding clear notes ensures a consistent, compliant, and fairly priced approach to demolition debris handling.
  37. The correct answer is A (Mulch Layer): a surface mulch layer conserves moisture, buffers soil temperature swings, and reduces weed competition at the root zone, but its depth and extent should be set based on actual field-verified conditions rather than assumed from an incomplete base plan. Accurate existing conditions information, including canopy and root zone data confirmed with the arborist, must be established before this and other planting details are finalized. Estimating conditions from an aerial photo risks specifying a mulch depth or extent that does not match the real tree. Correcting the base plan early avoids costly field changes or tree damage later.
  38. The correct answer is B (Relocate the fencing to preserve both tree protection and a functional staging area): protection measures and construction logistics must be coordinated so neither is compromised. Relocating the fence to avoid the staging conflict while still adequately protecting the tree's critical root zone resolves the issue without sacrificing either requirement.
  39. The correct answer is D (Drainage Aggregate with Perforated Pipe): this layer collects and conveys water away from the back of the wall, relieving the hydrostatic pressure that is a leading cause of retaining wall failure. If this drainage system is degraded, undersized, or blocked, an existing wall that has stood without visible failure could still be at risk once new construction changes surface drainage or surcharge loads. A structural evaluation should confirm this drainage layer, along with the wall itself, before the wall is shown to remain in the construction documents. Assuming adequacy based on wall age alone ignores this hidden risk.
  40. The correct answers are A and B: field verifying survey accuracy against visible features and confirming utility locates have been obtained are essential steps before an existing conditions plan becomes the reliable base for later sheets. Selecting furniture finishes or drafting a planting schedule are later design tasks unrelated to base plan verification.
  41. The correct answer is C (The landscape architect should coordinate with the electrical consultant and issue a clarified direction): conflicting information between consultants must be resolved through coordination, not left to the contractor's discretion. A clear, documented resolution avoids confusion in the field and ensures both disciplines' intent is properly reconciled.
  42. The correct answer is C (Base Course Matched to Existing): if the new base course thickness or material does not match the existing base at the saw-cut joint, the finished wearing course above it will settle unevenly, telegraphing a ridge or dip at the seam. Defining saw-cut limits and matching the underlying base course prevents this uneven edge and the disputes and change orders that follow from an ambiguous transition. A note lacking defined limits leaves the contractor guessing where the match line falls and how deep to match. Clear saw-cut and base-matching information supports a clean, predictable transition and consistent bidding.
  43. The correct answer is A (On the tree protection plan and referenced in the general notes for coordination): a root pruning requirement recommended by an arborist must be documented on the drawings so contractors performing nearby excavation are aware of it. Verbal communication or undocumented correspondence is insufficient to ensure compliance in the field.
  44. The correct answer is C (Critical Root Zone (Undisturbed Native Soil)): the critical root zone is the area of undisturbed soil surrounding a tree that must remain free of compaction, grade change, and excavation, and its extent is calculated from the tree's trunk diameter and canopy spread. A generic fence detail sized for an average tree will under-protect a larger specimen whose critical root zone extends further out. A QA/QC reviewer should confirm that each tree's protection detail is scaled to its own measurements rather than copied from a single generic detail.
