Because a bioswale conveys flow along its length while treating it, the point at the lowest elevation on the alignment is where filtered and infiltrated flow ultimately leaves the facility. Points A, C, and D sit at progressively higher elevations along the corridor, closer to the upper right corner of the plan, and represent locations upstream of the discharge point rather than the outlet itself. Correctly identifying the downstream end of a linear conveyance BMP on a grading plan is necessary to confirm the outlet condition matches the intended design.
  139. The correct answer is A (An engineered soil mix and shallow ponding depth that allow water to infiltrate within about a day): A rain garden depends on an engineered soil mix with adequate infiltration capacity and a shallow ponding depth designed to drain within roughly a day, so standing water does not persist long enough to create a mosquito breeding or plant health problem. A rain garden is not intended to hold a continuous, multi-foot-deep permanent pool, which would instead describe a retention pond. A fully lined, impermeable basin would defeat the entire purpose of an infiltration-based practice. Locating a rain garden over shallow bedrock with essentially no soil profile would prevent the infiltration the design depends on.
  140. The correct answer is B (A detention basin temporarily stores runoff and releases it at a controlled rate over time): A detention basin is designed to temporarily store incoming runoff and release it at a controlled rate over time, reducing the peak discharge reaching downstream systems, which is the fundamental function of this BMP type. A retention pond and a bioswale serve very different hydrologic functions and do not share an identical outlet control structure design. Detention and retention are not interchangeable names for the same facility, since a retention pond maintains a permanent pool while a detention basin is designed to empty out between storms. Distinguishing these two facility types correctly is essential when selecting the appropriate BMP for a project's peak rate control needs.

Omitting the intensity term and multiplying only C times A gives the incorrect 4.25 cfs distractor. Omitting the runoff coefficient and multiplying only i times A gives 25.0 cfs, a value far too high because it ignores land cover entirely. Carrying the correct composite coefficient forward into the peak flow equation is essential once it has been properly derived.
  142. The correct answer is C (Water infiltrates through the surface into an open-graded aggregate storage layer beneath the pavement): Permeable pavement allows rainfall to infiltrate through the paving surface itself into an open-graded aggregate storage layer beneath it, from which water either infiltrates further into the subgrade or is detained and released slowly. It is not simply a standard asphalt surface with a lighter reflective color, which would provide no hydrologic benefit at all. Because water passes through rather than sheeting across the top, permeable pavement does not rely on surface flow reaching a curb inlet the way conventional pavement does. This infiltration mechanism is what allows permeable pavement to count toward a site's water quality volume requirement.
  143. The correct answer is D (Excessive nutrient loading combined with insufficient pool depth and poor water circulation): Persistent algae blooms in a retention pond's permanent pool are most commonly caused by excessive nutrient loading combined with insufficient pool depth and poor internal water circulation, all of which promote excessive algal growth in stagnant, nutrient-rich water. A rapidly discharging outlet structure would tend to reduce residence time rather than promote algae growth. A properly sized forebay that captures incoming sediment actually helps water quality by reducing nutrient-laden sediment reaching the main pool. A planted littoral shelf around the pond edge also improves water quality through nutrient uptake rather than causing the problem described.
  144. Retention and detention performance varies significantly with growing medium depth and plant selection, so performance is not identical across all systems. An extensive green roof is specifically defined by its shallow depth, and requiring eighteen inches of soil would instead describe an intensive green roof system.
  145. It does not support a full soil and vegetation assembly, which instead describes a green roof. A blue roof does not store potable water for occupant consumption, which would be an entirely different rainwater harvesting system. A reflective white membrane alone, with no detention feature, would provide no hydrologic performance benefit and is not what defines a blue roof.
  146. The correct answer is C (A brown roof uses recycled substrate and self-colonizing vegetation to support local biodiversity and habitat): A brown roof uses recycled, often site-derived substrate and self-colonizing vegetation to create habitat conditions that support local biodiversity, which distinguishes it in intent from a conventional extensive green roof planted with a curated sedum palette chosen mainly for stormwater and aesthetic performance. A brown roof is a deliberately designed habitat system, not simply a green roof that has been abandoned and allowed to die. Its structural load is generally comparable to a typical extensive green roof, not to a saturated retention pond. A brown roof is not a parking surface and typically includes a vegetated, habitat-supporting component.

It does not provide stormwater performance equivalent to a properly sized below-grade detention vault, since a wall assembly has much less storage volume. Keeping the cistern permanently full for irrigation supply would leave no capacity available to capture and detain the next storm's runoff. Permanently sealing the outlet so no water is ever released for irrigation defeats the water supply purpose of the cistern entirely. Sizing the cistern without regard to projected irrigation demand ignores the actual water balance the system is meant to serve.
  150. The correct answer is A (Wastewater collected from bathroom sinks, showers, and clothes washing machines): Graywater suitable for landscape irrigation reuse is generally defined as wastewater collected from sources such as bathroom sinks, showers, and clothes washing machines, which carry a relatively low pathogen load compared to other wastewater streams. Toilet discharge is classified as blackwater and is routed to the sanitary sewer rather than reused as graywater. Stormwater runoff from a parking lot is an entirely separate waste stream governed by different regulations and is not graywater. Commercial kitchen dishwashing wastewater typically carries higher grease and food solid content and is generally excluded from residential graywater reuse definitions under most codes.

It is not defined as the runoff from the single largest storm ever recorded in a region, which instead relates to flood control design storms. It also has no relationship to a retention pond's permanent pool volume or to a building's annual potable water consumption. Capturing and treating this first flush volume before discharge is the specific purpose most water quality BMPs are sized to achieve.

Erosion controls are required from the start of land disturbance, not only after the site has been fully paved, since exposed soil is most vulnerable to erosion during active construction.

There is no exemption from this requirement simply because a site is smaller than ten acres, since the federal threshold is generally set at one acre. A building occupancy permit addresses an entirely different regulatory process and does not satisfy stormwater discharge permitting requirements. A residential wastewater discharge permit issued by a local health department is also unrelated to construction stormwater discharge regulation.

A final plat recordation document is a separate legal instrument related to subdivision of land and has no relationship to construction stormwater permitting. A certificate of substantial completion is issued by the architect of record for construction contract administration purposes. A Phase I Environmental Site Assessment is a due diligence document prepared for property transactions and is unrelated to the NPDES construction permitting process.

Because a SWPPP must be actively maintained and updated as construction phasing changes, erosion and sediment controls need to be relocated or supplemented at the low point currently receiving disturbed-area runoff, rather than left only at the original phase's control locations. Points A, C, and D sit at different elevations on the plan that do not correspond to the newly active low point requiring updated controls. Correctly identifying where flow is now concentrating on the current grading phase is necessary before SWPPP measures can be properly updated.
  160. The correct answers are A and B: As-built drawings confirming that permanent BMPs were constructed as approved are commonly required to close out the post-construction stormwater review, and a long-term maintenance agreement obligating the owner to maintain those facilities is also a typical requirement before final approval or occupancy. Post-construction stormwater requirements are separate from, and do not automatically terminate alongside, the NPDES construction permit, which governs only the active construction period. Occupancy permits are frequently conditioned on demonstrating that required stormwater facilities have actually been completed and function as designed, not left unconditioned.
  161. The correct answer is C (0.33): The composite, area-weighted runoff coefficient equals the sum of each sub-area's C times its area, divided by total area: (2 times 0.90, plus 3 times 0.25, plus 5 times 0.15), divided by 10 acres. That is (1.8 plus 0.75 plus 0.75) divided by 10, which equals 3.3 divided by 10, or 0.33. A simple, unweighted average of the three raw C values would incorrectly produce roughly 0.43, ignoring that the wooded area contributes half the total acreage. Correctly weighting each land cover type by its actual area is required whenever more than two sub-areas of differing size are combined.
  162. The correct answer is A (Reducing overall parking stall dimensions and using shared parking to shrink the paved footprint): Reducing overall parking stall dimensions and implementing shared parking arrangements directly shrinks the total paved footprint required on a site, which is the most direct way to reduce total impervious cover generated by a development. Widening internal drive aisles beyond the minimum required for emergency access increases paved area rather than reducing it. Replacing a planted median with additional continuous asphalt increases impervious cover. Eliminating on-site bioretention areas to allow a larger building footprint also works against, rather than toward, the goal of minimizing impervious cover.
  163. The correct answer is A (Limiting the post-development peak discharge to no more than the pre-development peak discharge rate): A detention basin's outlet structure is typically designed to limit the post-development peak discharge rate to no more than the pre-development peak discharge rate for the applicable design storms, which is the standard release rate control objective most jurisdictions require. Releasing the entire stored volume as quickly as possible after each storm would defeat the purpose of temporary storage and would not control the peak rate at all. Maintaining a constant discharge rate regardless of stored volume is not how a properly designed outlet structure, such as an orifice or weir, actually behaves hydraulically. Discharging only during dry periods elsewhere in the region has no relationship to how a detention basin's outlet is designed to function.
  164. The correct answer is D (An underground infiltration gallery or chamber system beneath a parking area): An underground infiltration gallery or chamber system beneath a parking area allows a site with good native soil infiltration capacity but limited available surface area to manage stormwater without consuming additional land for a surface facility. A large open surface retention pond would require substantial surface area that the site does not have available. A constructed wetland similarly requires extensive surface area and shallow standing water that is inconsistent with the stated site constraint. A vegetated bioswale also requires a continuous linear surface corridor, which conflicts with the limited surface area described in this scenario.
  165. The correct answer is C (The basin alternates between temporary ponding after storms and dry conditions between rain events): A rain garden basin alternates between temporary ponding shortly after a storm event and dry conditions during the periods between rain events, so its plant palette must tolerate both extremes to survive over the long term. Rain gardens are specifically designed to drain within about a day and are not permanently flooded year round. Well-designed rain gardens are, in fact, expected to periodically hold standing water after storms, contrary to a claim that they never experience inundation. Plant selection is directly tied to a rain garden's hydrologic regime, since a species intolerant of either extreme will eventually fail in that setting.
  166. The correct answer is B (Capturing coarse sediment before it enters the main pond, easing future maintenance): A forebay placed at the inlet of a retention pond primarily functions to capture coarser sediment before it reaches and accumulates in the main pond, which significantly eases future dredging and maintenance of the larger facility. It is not the pond's water supply source, since a retention pond's permanent pool is typically maintained through a combination of inflow and a controlled outlet elevation, not the forebay itself. The forebay does not serve as the pond's outlet control structure, which is a separate design element governing release rate. A forebay also does not replace the water quality benefit of a vegetated buffer around the pond's perimeter.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (To convey treated water out of the cell when infiltration through the native soil is too slow): An underdrain conveys treated water out of a bioretention cell when the surrounding native clay soil infiltrates too slowly to fully drain the cell within the design timeframe, preventing prolonged ponding that could otherwise stress plantings or create a nuisance. An underdrain is not an irrigation supply line pushing water up into the media from below. Its purpose is the opposite of preventing water from ever leaving the cell, since it is specifically designed to allow controlled drainage. Including an underdrain does not eliminate the need for an engineered soil media layer, which still provides filtration and supports plant growth.
  2. The correct answer is A (13.2 cfs): Using the previously calculated composite C of 0.33 for the 10 acre site, Q equals C times i times A, so Q equals 0.33 times 4.0 in/hr times 10 acres, which equals 13.2 cfs. Omitting the runoff coefficient and multiplying only i times A gives the incorrect 40.0 cfs distractor. Omitting intensity and multiplying only C times A gives 3.3 cfs, understating the peak flow substantially. Omitting area and multiplying only C times i gives 1.32 cfs, which is far too low for a 10 acre contributing drainage area.
  3. The correct answer is D (The growing medium absorbs and retains a significant portion of small rainfall depths before any runoff occurs): A green roof's growing medium absorbs and retains a meaningful portion of the rainfall from small, frequent storm events before any runoff is generated, which is why it typically outperforms a conventional roof for these common, low-intensity events. A green roof does not shed one hundred percent of every rainfall event immediately, since retention within the medium is precisely its stormwater benefit. A conventional built-up roof, lacking any retention medium, generally sheds nearly all rainfall as runoff and does not outperform a vegetated roof. For storms under about half an inch, a green roof's retention capacity is often sufficient to capture the entire event with little to no runoff.
  4. The correct answers are A and B: harvested rainwater stored in a cistern can offset potable water demand for non-potable uses such as landscape irrigation, and graywater systems typically exclude high-pathogen wastewater sources such as toilet discharge, which is instead classified as blackwater. Cisterns and graywater systems are governed by different code provisions addressing different water sources and are not regulated identically everywhere. A properly designed cistern does provide a peak-flow reduction benefit, since captured rainfall is detained and released later rather than immediately contributing to peak runoff.
  5. The correct answer is A (Providing adequate storage in an open-graded aggregate reservoir layer sized to the design storm): Providing adequate storage in an open-graded aggregate reservoir layer, sized to the selected design storm, is the most critical subgrade design consideration for permeable pavement's long-term hydrologic performance, since this layer is what actually stores infiltrating water. Compacting the subgrade to the same density used for a conventional impervious pavement section would reduce infiltration capacity and defeat the purpose of the permeable system. Eliminating the geotextile separator layer would allow fine subgrade soil to migrate into and clog the aggregate reservoir over time. Sloping the surface to drain rapidly to a storm drain inlet works against the intended infiltration function of the system.
  6. The correct answer is B (A vegetated buffer helps filter sediment and nutrients from sheet flow before it reaches open water): A vegetated buffer surrounding a constructed wetland helps filter sediment and nutrients out of incoming sheet flow before that flow reaches the open water of the wetland, improving overall water quality performance. Requiring every buffer to exceed one hundred feet regardless of the BMP it surrounds is not a universal standard, since required buffer widths vary by jurisdiction and by BMP type. Claiming the buffer provides no measurable water quality benefit contradicts its documented filtering function. Framing the buffer as purely aesthetic ignores its established role in pollutant removal and edge stabilization.
  7. Because any runoff entering a site from upstream, including flow conveyed through an existing culvert, must be safely conveyed through or around the proposed development, correctly locating this entry point is necessary to design the onsite conveyance system that will carry it to a safe outlet. Points A, B, and C sit at lower elevations on the plan and represent locations downstream of this entry point rather than the point where offsite flow first reaches the site. Correctly reading the contour pattern to find where upstream flow enters the site is a foundational step before that offsite area can be included in the overall drainage analysis.

As flow travels from this upper point down the length of the corridor toward its lower discharge end, filtration, infiltration, and biological uptake occur along the way, which is what gives the bioswale its superior pollutant removal performance compared to a simple grass-lined ditch. Points A, B, and D sit at lower elevations along or below the corridor and represent locations downstream of the inlet rather than the point where flow first enters the facility. Correctly identifying the upstream inlet on the grading plan is necessary to confirm the treatment length available before the outlet point is reached.

Omitting the runoff coefficient and multiplying only i times A gives the incorrect 21.0 cfs distractor.   10. The correct answer is A (Different storm frequencies stress different aspects of the drainage system, from minor conveyance to major flood control): Different storm frequencies stress different aspects of a drainage system, from everyday minor conveyance sized for a small, frequent storm to major flood control facilities sized for a rare, high-intensity event, which is why most stormwater ordinances require analysis across multiple design storm return periods. A single storm frequency does not govern the sizing of every system element, since a pipe network and a detention basin's emergency spillway often respond to largely different design storms. Larger storm frequencies are analyzed for engineering capacity, not for planting selection. Design storm frequency is central to both detention basin sizing and outlet structure design, not unrelated to them.
  11. The correct answer is C (Treating runoff closer to its source can improve water quality and reduce the size of downstream conveyance): Treating runoff closer to its source through a series of small, distributed bioretention cells can improve overall water quality performance while also reducing the size of downstream conveyance piping needed to carry flow to a single collection point. A distributed approach does not inherently cost significantly more than a single large detention basin and, depending on site conditions, can sometimes reduce overall infrastructure cost. Distributed bioretention cells still require some level of conveyance piping between cells and outfalls, so the approach does not eliminate piping needs entirely. Small, well-designed bioretention cells provide a genuine water quality benefit rather than none at all.
  12. The correct answers are A and B: curb extension bioretention can capture street runoff and treat it before that flow ever reaches an existing storm drain inlet, and combining multiple green infrastructure practices into a single treatment train, as in this streetscape retrofit, can improve overall pollutant removal compared with any single practice alone. Permeable pavement crosswalks are engineered to support pedestrian and, in many applications, vehicular loading, so claiming they cannot support foot traffic is incorrect. Adding bioretention curb extensions does not eliminate the need to coordinate lighting or signage relocation where those elements conflict with the new planting areas.
  13. The correct answer is B (A Clean Water Act Section 404 permit if the project affects a jurisdictional wetland or waterway): A Clean Water Act Section 404 permit may be required beyond the NPDES Construction General Permit if the proposed stormwater wetland or associated construction activity affects a jurisdictional wetland or other water of the United States. A building code occupancy classification review conducted by the fire marshal addresses life safety code compliance and is unrelated to wetland impacts. A historic preservation review is not automatically triggered by every stormwater facility proposal. An aviation obstruction permit applies to structures that could pose a hazard to air navigation and has no relationship to a low-lying stormwater wetland.
  14. Starting the blanket at the top of the slope and anchoring it in a trenched key at that high point prevents water from getting underneath the blanket and undermining it as flow moves downhill toward the lower points. Points A, B, and C sit at lower elevations, farther down the slope, and represent locations the blanket would extend to and overlap through rather than its starting anchor point. Correctly identifying the top of the slope on the grading plan is necessary before an erosion control blanket installation detail can be properly sequenced.

The landscape architect does not independently issue the jurisdiction's final permit approval, which remains the reviewing agency's regulatory function. Structural design of a reinforced concrete outlet structure is typically the responsibility of a licensed structural or civil engineer. Certifying soil percolation test results is the responsibility of a licensed geotechnical professional, not the landscape architect.

Salt tolerance is most critical for plantings at this upstream location, since repeated exposure to concentrated chloride-laden runoff can otherwise cause dieback and gradual loss of BMP function before any dilution occurs farther down the swale. Points A, C, and D sit at lower elevations farther from the roadway inlet, where runoff has already traveled and diluted somewhat as it moves toward the downstream end of the facility.   18. The correct answer is A (Additional pollutant uptake and habitat value while helping stabilize the pond edge against erosion): A littoral shelf planted with emergent aquatic vegetation around a retention pond's perimeter primarily provides additional pollutant uptake and habitat value while also helping stabilize the pond edge against wave action and erosion. It is not a structural foundation supporting the pond's primary outlet control structure, which is a separate engineered element. A littoral shelf is intentionally shallow and vegetated, not a permanently dry walking surface for pedestrian access. It also does not replace the sediment-capture role of a forebay located at the pond's inlet, since the two features serve different functions.
  19. The correct answer is D (An extensive green roof system, which uses a shallow growing medium and lightweight drought-tolerant plants): An extensive green roof system, with its shallow growing medium and lightweight, drought-tolerant plant selection, imposes a much lower additional dead load on the roof structure than a deep-soil system, making it the appropriate choice where structural capacity is limited. An intensive green roof, supporting trees, shrubs, and deep soil for active roof gardens, requires substantially greater structural capacity than an extensive system. A rooftop pool holding several feet of standing water across the entire roof plate would impose an even greater load than either type of green roof. A rooftop mechanical yard with heavy equipment and no vegetation also does not address the structural constraint in a way that provides any stormwater benefit.
  20. The correct answers are A and B: Perimeter erosion controls, such as silt fence, should be installed and functional before mass grading operations begin, and sediment basins should be constructed early enough in the sequence to capture runoff from the very first areas disturbed on the site. Installing erosion controls only after all impervious surfaces have already been paved contradicts accepted sequencing, since soil is most vulnerable during the earliest stages of disturbance. Removing perimeter controls as soon as rough grading is finished, before permanent vegetation has established, would leave exposed soil unprotected during a period of continued erosion risk.
  21. The correct answer is C (The fence fabric was not properly trenched and keyed into the ground along its entire length): Sediment bypassing an installed silt fence during a storm most commonly results from the fence fabric not being properly trenched and keyed into the ground along its entire length, allowing water and sediment to flow beneath the fence rather than being filtered by it. Installing the fence parallel to the contour along the downslope perimeter is actually the correct installation practice, not a deficiency. Fence height that meets the manufacturer's recommended maximum is also a proper installation detail, not a cause of failure. Installing the fence before grading activity begins is likewise the correct sequencing, not the source of the bypass problem described.
  22. Limiting inspections to only the winter months would leave the site unmonitored during other seasons when significant storms still occur. Requiring only a single inspection immediately before the certificate of occupancy would fail to catch problems throughout the active construction period. Inspection responsibility generally extends to designated qualified personnel on the contractor's team, not exclusively to the design engineer.

  24. Routing overflow directly into the building's potable water supply piping would create a serious cross-connection and public health hazard. Recirculating overflow back into the same downspout that feeds the cistern would create a closed loop with nowhere for excess water to actually go. Routing stormwater overflow into the sanitary sewer, rather than an approved stormwater discharge point, is generally prohibited and can overload sanitary treatment infrastructure.

Because higher impervious cover directly increases both runoff volume and peak flow rate, the BMP located at this low point must be sized to accommodate the elevated runoff coefficient produced by the paved area upstream. Points B, C, and D sit at higher elevations across the site and represent locations where runoff is generated and conveyed from, rather than the concentration point where the facility itself is sited. Correctly identifying the site's low point on the grading plan is necessary before a BMP sizing calculation can confirm compliance with the jurisdiction's impervious cover limit.
  26. The correct answer is B (When the pond is sized with adequate permanent pool volume and detention capacity to meet both standards): A retention pond can generally satisfy both detention and water quality objectives when it is sized with an adequate permanent pool volume, which provides pollutant settling and biological treatment, combined with additional temporary storage capacity above that pool to control peak discharge rate. Claiming a retention pond can never provide water quality treatment contradicts its established permanent-pool treatment mechanism. A retention pond does not automatically satisfy water quality requirements regardless of its size, since an undersized pool would fail to provide adequate treatment residence time. Water quality treatment can be achieved through several BMP types, not exclusively through an underground vault system.
  27. The correct answer is C (Rain barrels have relatively small storage capacity and fill quickly during moderate to large storm events): Rain barrels have relatively small storage capacity compared with a properly sized cistern and typically fill quickly during moderate to large storm events, limiting how much runoff volume they can meaningfully capture and detain. Rain barrels cannot store significantly more volume than a properly sized underground cistern, which is generally designed with far greater capacity. Rain barrels are filled by roof runoff, not by a continuous connection to the municipal potable water supply. Because they fill quickly, rain barrels still require a downstream overflow path to safely handle runoff once their limited capacity has been exceeded during a large storm.
  28. The correct answers are A and B: a bioretention cell's cross section typically includes an engineered soil media layer designed to both support plant growth and provide infiltration or filtration of incoming runoff, and a surface mulch layer that helps retain moisture and suppress weed growth around the plantings. An impervious plastic liner is not always required beneath every bioretention cell, and is instead used selectively where infiltration must be prevented, such as near a building foundation or contaminated soil. Bioretention cells are typically designed with an overflow structure to safely convey flow from storms that exceed the cell's design capacity, not without one.
  29. The correct answer is B (Sediment loading can clog the permeable surface over time, reducing its infiltration capacity): Sediment loading from an adjacent unpaved area can clog the surface voids of permeable pavement over time, progressively reducing its infiltration capacity and eventually causing the system to function more like a conventional impervious surface. Permeable pavement does not structurally outperform conventional asphalt under every traffic loading condition, and heavy truck traffic in particular requires careful structural design consideration. Sediment does have a measurable, well-documented negative effect on permeable pavement's long-term infiltration performance. Heavy truck traffic combined with sediment-laden runoff tends to degrade, not improve, a permeable system's infiltration rate over time.

Dividing Q by intensity alone while ignoring area gives the incorrect 2.25 shown as a distractor. Dividing by an incorrectly larger product, such as including an extra factor of area, would understate C to the incorrect 0.25 shown as another distractor. Simply repeating the given flow value of 6.75 as if it were the coefficient ignores that C must be a dimensionless ratio, not a flow rate.
  31. The correct answer is C (Subsurface distribution that prevents direct human contact with the graywater during application): Subsurface distribution of graywater for irrigation prevents direct human contact with the water during application, which is the primary public health protection typically required by local plumbing code for this type of reuse system. Above-ground spray irrigation directly onto edible food crops would create a direct human exposure pathway to graywater, which is generally restricted or prohibited by code. A direct cross-connection between a graywater system and the potable water supply is a serious code violation and public health hazard. Storing untreated graywater in an open, uncovered reservoir for extended periods creates odor, mosquito breeding, and public health concerns that subsurface systems are designed to avoid.
  32. The correct answer is D (To safely convey overflow during a storm larger than the basin's design capacity without overtopping the embankment): A detention basin's emergency spillway safely conveys overflow during a storm larger than the basin's design capacity, preventing uncontrolled overtopping of the embankment that could otherwise lead to embankment failure and downstream flooding. The emergency spillway is not the everyday primary discharge path, which is instead handled by the basin's primary outlet structure for typical, more frequent storm events. It does not function as a year-round permanent water supply source unrelated to storm size. An emergency spillway supplements, rather than replaces, the need for a properly designed primary outlet control structure.
  33. The correct answer is A (Different plant species tolerate different depths and durations of inundation, so matching species to zone ensures survival): Different plant species tolerate different depths and durations of inundation, so matching species to the appropriate hydrology zone, from permanently inundated to occasionally saturated, is essential to ensuring long-term survival and BMP function across a stormwater wetland's varied conditions. Claiming all wetland species tolerate identical hydrologic conditions ignores well-documented differences in plant hydrology tolerance. Planting zones are directly tied to a wetland's actual hydrologic performance, since they reflect where and how long water is expected to be present. A properly designed stormwater wetland maintains varying water depths across zones rather than a single uniform depth everywhere.
  34. The correct answer is D (Tree canopy intercepts rainfall and promotes evapotranspiration, reducing the volume of runoff reaching the ground): Tree canopy intercepts a portion of incoming rainfall on leaves and branches and promotes evapotranspiration, both of which reduce the volume of rainfall that ultimately reaches the ground as runoff, which is why preserved canopy can earn credit toward a site's stormwater management obligation. Claiming canopy has no measurable effect on interception or runoff volume contradicts well-established hydrologic research on forest canopy effects. Preserved trees reduce, rather than increase, a site's effective impervious cover contribution to runoff calculations. Tree canopy credit in a stormwater manual is tied to runoff reduction, not to a site's required parking stall count.
  35. A range of 0.05 to 0.10 describes an undisturbed forested condition, which is far more permeable than compacted gravel. A range of 0.90 to 0.95 describes a fully impervious roof surface, which sheds far more runoff than a gravel drive. Assigning an exact value of 1.00 would treat gravel as completely impervious, which does not reflect its actual infiltration behavior.

Obtaining approval from one agency does not automatically satisfy the separate requirements of the other agency, since each reviews different regulatory obligations. A project generally cannot proceed to construction until both required approvals have actually been obtained, not merely one of the two.

As-built surveys are a standard, not merely optional, part of stormwater facility acceptance for most jurisdictions. An as-built survey documents completed construction but does not replace the need for ongoing maintenance of the facility over its service life. As-built verification requirements commonly extend to constructed stormwater BMPs, not solely to buildings.
  40. The correct answer is A (Periodic vacuum sweeping or pressure washing to remove accumulated fine sediment from the surface voids): Periodic vacuum sweeping or pressure washing to remove accumulated fine sediment from the surface voids is essential to sustaining a permeable pavement system's long-term infiltration function, since clogged voids progressively reduce the surface's ability to accept water. Sealing the surface annually with a standard impervious asphalt sealcoat would defeat the entire purpose of the permeable system by blocking infiltration. Allowing sediment and debris to accumulate indefinitely without scheduled cleaning would accelerate the clogging problem this maintenance activity is meant to prevent. Applying deicing sand liberally without any spring cleanup would introduce additional fine material that further reduces infiltration capacity over time.
  41. A dry detention basin, which drains completely between storms and lacks sustained vegetation contact with water, does not generally remove more dissolved nutrients than a properly functioning constructed wetland. Nutrient removal performance is not identical between the two facility types, since their hydrologic and vegetative characteristics differ substantially. A constructed wetland provides documented water quality benefits well beyond what a simple paved detention facility offers.

Underground vaults do not automatically provide superior water quality treatment compared with a vegetated BMP, since they lack biological and soil-based treatment mechanisms. Water quality treatment is still generally required on sites that include a detention vault, since detention and treatment are separate design objectives. Pretreatment devices are also commonly required for subsurface systems, not exclusively for surface BMPs.

The correct answer is C (5 in/hr): Rearranging the Rational Method formula to solve for i gives i equals Q divided by the product of C and A, so i equals 10.0 divided by the product of 0.5 and 4.0, which is 10.0 divided by 2.0, equaling 5 in/hr. Dividing Q by an incomplete product that omits area gives the incorrect 2 in/hr distractor. An arbitrary value such as 1.25 does not result from correctly applying the rearranged formula to the given values. Multiplying rather than dividing Q by C and A gives 20, an inflated value produced by applying the wrong arithmetic operation to solve for intensity.
  45. The correct answer is C (The cistern reduces runoff volume reaching downstream systems while offsetting potable irrigation demand): A rainwater harvesting cistern reduces the volume of runoff reaching downstream stormwater systems while simultaneously offsetting a portion of potable water demand used for irrigation, making it a genuine dual-purpose contributor within a site's broader stormwater management strategy. A cistern does not eliminate the need for other stormwater practices on a site, since it typically manages only a fraction of the total contributing runoff. Cisterns and sanitary sewer connections are governed by entirely different code provisions and are not regulated identically. A properly designed cistern provides a measurable hydrologic benefit and is far more than a purely architectural feature.
  46. The correct answers are A and B: a riparian buffer adjacent to a perennial stream should generally remain undisturbed and should be clearly delineated with protective fencing before construction activity begins nearby, and construction staging and material stockpiling should be located outside that delineated buffer area to avoid inadvertent disturbance. Permanently clearing a riparian buffer, even if erosion control blankets are installed afterward, does not satisfy the intent of a mandated buffer meant to remain vegetated. Riparian buffer requirements commonly apply to both residential and commercial development, not exclusively to one project type.
  47. The correct answer is A (Water quality volume targets pollutant treatment for frequent small storms, while rate control targets peak flow for larger events): The water quality volume targets pollutant treatment for the frequent, smaller storms that carry the highest concentration of pollutants, while a separate peak rate control requirement targets controlling the peak discharge rate for larger, less frequent design storms, meaning a basin sized only for the first objective would not necessarily satisfy the second. These two objectives are not simply two names for the same design requirement, since they address different hydrologic concerns with different sizing methods. Peak rate control requirements are not limited to sites smaller than one acre and commonly apply across a wide range of project sizes. Meeting the water quality volume requirement does not automatically satisfy a separate peak rate control requirement.
  48. The correct answer is D (The depth and water-holding capacity of the growing medium specified for the roof assembly): The depth and water-holding capacity of the growing medium specified for a green roof assembly most directly determines how much rainfall the system can retain, which is why this factor drives the stormwater retention credit the roof receives under a sustainability rating system. The exterior facade paint color selected for the ground floor has no relationship to the green roof's hydrologic performance. The number of parking spaces in an underground garage is unrelated to the rooftop's water retention capacity. The square footage of occupied interior office space also has no bearing on how much rainfall the green roof assembly can retain.
  49. The correct answer is B (Limiting the area of exposed soil at any one time reduces the potential volume of eroded sediment): Limiting the area of exposed soil at any one time through phased construction sequencing reduces the potential volume of sediment that can be generated and transported off site during any given period of active disturbance. Phasing construction does not increase total soil disturbance compared with grading an entire site at once, and in fact is intended to reduce the exposed area at any given moment. Phased sequencing does not eliminate the need for erosion control measures on the currently active phase, since that area is still actively disturbed and vulnerable. Construction phasing has a direct relationship to the quantity of sediment generated, which is precisely why it is used as an erosion control strategy.
  50. The correct answer is B (During active construction when sediment-laden sheet flow may reach a functioning storm drain inlet): A sediment trap combined with a filter fabric wrap around a curb inlet is most appropriate during active construction, when sediment-laden sheet flow from disturbed areas may reach a functioning storm drain inlet and needs to be filtered before entering the pipe system. This type of protection is not needed once a site has been fully stabilized with permanent vegetation and no further disturbance is planned, since the sediment source has been eliminated. It is similarly unnecessary on a fully paved, finished site where no further earthwork is occurring. A project with no storm drain inlets within the disturbed construction limits would have no inlet to protect using this measure.
  51. The correct answer is C (0.70): The composite, area-weighted runoff coefficient equals (5 acres times 0.90, plus 5 acres times 0.50), divided by 10 acres total. That is (4.5 plus 2.5) divided by 10, which equals 7.0 divided by 10, or 0.70. Using only the pavement's coefficient of 0.90 while ignoring the gravel area, or only the gravel's coefficient of 0.50 while ignoring the pavement area, both fail to account for the full site. Simply adding the two raw coefficients together without dividing by total area gives the incorrect 1.40, which is not even a physically valid runoff coefficient since C cannot exceed 1.0.
  52. The correct answer is D (Whether the bioswale can safely convey the design storm flow without eroding or overtopping its banks): Whether a bioswale can safely convey the design storm flow without eroding its banks or overtopping is the governing consideration when deciding whether it can substitute for a required curb and gutter conveyance system along a roadway. The presence of visibly flowering plant species has no bearing on the swale's hydraulic conveyance capacity. The specific brand of geotextile fabric beneath the engineered soil is a construction detail unrelated to whether the substitution is hydraulically appropriate. Whether the adjacent pavement has standard traffic striping is also unrelated to the bioswale's ability to convey the design flow safely.
  53. The correct answer is C (The comparison demonstrates whether the proposed development increases runoff volume or peak flow beyond allowed limits): Comparing modeled pre-development and post-development hydrology demonstrates whether the proposed development increases runoff volume or peak flow beyond what the jurisdiction's stormwater ordinance allows, which is the core purpose of this required comparison. Pre-development conditions remain directly relevant throughout the approval process and are not rendered irrelevant once preliminary subdivision approval has been granted. Post-development modeling is required for essentially all developments that increase impervious surface, not only for sites with no impervious area at all. Municipal reviewers routinely compare pre- and post-development flows as a standard part of stormwater plan review.
  54. The correct answers are A and B: a stormwater management report typically includes a description of the proposed BMPs along with supporting sizing calculations for each facility, and a summary of pre- and post-development peak discharge rates for each analyzed design storm, both of which demonstrate the proposed system meets applicable requirements. The personal home addresses of subcontractors have no relevance to a stormwater engineering submittal and would not appear in this type of report. A complete interior finish schedule and furniture specification package similarly belongs to an entirely different discipline's submittal and is unrelated to stormwater compliance documentation.
  55. The correct answer is A (Shallow bedrock limits available infiltration depth, which can cause the rain garden to pond longer than intended): Shallow bedrock limits the available depth for infiltration beneath a rain garden, which can cause the basin to pond for a longer duration than intended and potentially exceed the roughly one-day drawdown period the design relies on. Shallow bedrock does not increase the infiltration rate available to a bioretention practice, since bedrock is essentially impermeable compared with the overlying soil. Bedrock depth has a direct and significant bearing on the hydrologic performance of any infiltration-based BMP placed above it. A rain garden does not perform identically whether placed over shallow bedrock or over deep, well-drained soil, which is precisely why a geotechnical investigation should precede BMP siting.
  56. The correct answer is C (Sediment-laden runoff from an unstabilized drainage area can clog and shorten the service life of the BMP): Sediment-laden runoff generated by an unstabilized contributing drainage area can clog a downstream BMP's surface or media, shortening its effective service life, which is why permanent BMPs are typically sequenced to come online only after their contributing area has been stabilized. Claiming a permanent BMP performs better when receiving sediment-laden runoff during early construction phases contradicts the well-documented clogging risk this sequencing rule is meant to avoid. Stabilization of the contributing area has a direct effect on a downstream BMP's long-term infiltration capacity, not none at all. Permanent BMPs should generally be placed into service after, not before, grading of their contributing area has occurred.
  57. The correct answer is B (A value in the range of about 0.15 to 0.30): A well-maintained turf lawn on flat, well-drained soil typically falls within a C value range of about 0.15 to 0.30, reflecting its moderate infiltration capacity compared with more impervious surfaces. A range of 0.90 to 0.95 describes a fully impervious surface such as pavement or roofing, which is far less permeable than turf. A range of 0.50 to 0.65 would overstate turf's runoff behavior under typical flat, well-drained conditions. Assigning an exact value of 1.00 would treat turf as completely impervious, which does not reflect its actual hydrologic behavior.
  58. The correct answer is B (21.0 cfs): Using the previously calculated composite C of 0.70 for the 10 acre site, Q equals C times i times A, so Q equals 0.70 times 3.0 in/hr times 10 acres, which equals 21.0 cfs. Omitting the intensity term and multiplying only C times A gives the incorrect 7.0 cfs distractor. Omitting the runoff coefficient and multiplying only i times A gives 30.0 cfs, which overstates the true peak flow because it ignores land cover. Omitting area and multiplying only C times i gives 2.1 cfs, far too low to represent a 10 acre contributing drainage area.
  59. The correct answer is A (Relatively small drainage areas where peak flow, rather than a full runoff hydrograph, is the primary design need): The Rational Method is generally considered appropriate for relatively small drainage areas where the primary design need is a single peak flow value rather than a full runoff hydrograph describing flow over time. It is not appropriate for very large regional watersheds spanning many square miles with highly variable land cover, where a hydrograph method better captures the complexity of the system. A drainage area covered entirely by a single, continuous permanent water body would not be an appropriate application of a runoff-based method at all. The Rational Method's assumptions do not hold equally across all watershed scales, which is why its use is generally limited to smaller drainage areas.
  60. The correct answers are A and B: an approved erosion and sediment control plan is commonly required before a local grading permit can be issued, and evidence of NPDES Construction General Permit coverage is commonly required for any qualifying disturbed area before grading begins. Grading permits are not issued automatically without any review of proposed stormwater or erosion controls, since jurisdictions typically require demonstrated compliance first. A completed certificate of occupancy is issued near the end of a project, well after grading, and is not a prerequisite for obtaining a grading permit at the start of construction.
  61. The correct answer is D (A single BMP or combination of BMPs must be verified to meet every applicable requirement, not just one in isolation): A single BMP or combination of BMPs must be verified to meet every applicable requirement, including water quality, channel protection, and flood control, rather than assuming that satisfying one requirement automatically satisfies the others, since each addresses a distinct hydrologic concern. Meeting a flood control requirement does not automatically satisfy a separate water quality volume requirement, since the two are sized using different criteria and target different storm frequencies. These requirements are typically reviewed together as part of one integrated stormwater management plan, not by entirely unrelated sections of the ordinance with no overlap. Water quality, channel protection, and flood control requirements are commonly applied simultaneously on the same project, not mutually exclusive of one another.

The maximum running slope permitted on an accessible route is 1:20, which is exactly 5 percent, so this segment is compliant. Redrafting from scratch, sending unreviewed work directly to the client, and checking cosmetic formatting before substantive content are all less effective as a first review step.

Option A incorrectly scales the calculated result by a factor of ten.

  117. The correct answer is A (A perforated pipe in free draining gravel, sloped to an outlet away from the wall): a functional French drain uses a perforated pipe surrounded by permeable gravel to collect groundwater, with a continuous positive slope carrying that water to a safe outlet point away from the structure. A solid pipe, a pipe sloped in the wrong direction, or a pipe wrapped in impermeable material would each defeat the drain's purpose of collecting and conveying groundwater away from the wall.
  118. The correct answer is A (Raise finish grade near the tree, or add a tree well, keeping the root flare exposed): burying a tree's root flare below finished grade can suffocate roots and seriously harm the tree over time, so the appropriate response is to hold grade higher near the tree, often using a tree well or retaining curb, rather than burying the flare. Lowering grade to expose roots or ignoring the conflict both risk damaging the tree, and relocating the entire plan is an extreme step not required to resolve a single localized grading conflict.

Option B misplaces the decimal point, overstating the actual slope by a factor of ten.
  120. The correct answer is B (The swale can provide some infiltration, filtration, and flow attenuation as water moves across it): a vegetated swale allows some stormwater to infiltrate into the ground and be filtered by vegetation while also slowing the rate of flow, benefits that an enclosed pipe does not provide since it simply conveys water without treatment. Swale design still requires runoff calculations, relative construction costs vary by site, and pipe capacity depends on its specific size and slope, not on a blanket comparison to swales.

  122. Statement C is false because contour shape follows actual terrain, not property lines, and statement D is false because a contour line represents one fixed elevation value regardless of the viewer's orientation.
  123. A crown centered on the drain, a perfectly flat surface, and randomly placed high and low points would each work against reliably directing flow to the intended drain.
  124.   125. The correct answer is A (A detention basin briefly stores runoff, while an infiltration basin lets runoff soak into the ground): a detention basin controls peak discharge by holding water briefly and releasing it at a controlled rate through an outlet, while an infiltration basin is designed with permeable soils specifically to let stormwater percolate into the ground rather than being piped away. Both facility types can be constructed above or below grade, both typically include some form of outlet or overflow, and neither is limited to irrigation storage or sanitary sewage management.
  126. The correct answer is C (A 1:1 slope in loose sandy soil is likely too steep to remain stable): loose, sandy soils generally cannot maintain a steep 1:1, or 45 degree, cut slope without significant risk of sloughing or failure, so a geotechnical review and likely a flatter slope or retaining structure are warranted. A steep slope is not automatically a drafting error, has no bearing on the ADA pedestrian route standard, and slope stability is a genuine engineering concern, not merely an aesthetic one.
  127. The correct answer is D (Adjust the sidewalk profile to meet the fixed utility elevations while keeping a compliant overall slope): since the hydrant and vault lid cannot move, the sidewalk profile must be adjusted to tie into their fixed elevations smoothly while still meeting overall accessible slope requirements along the route. Ignoring the conflict, unilaterally requiring the utility company to relocate fixed infrastructure, or avoiding spot elevations near utilities are not realistic or appropriate responses to a genuine grading conflict.
  128. The correct answer is B (An overflow point set at an elevation that safely directs excess water away before flooding occurs): A rain garden needs a defined overflow elevation so that runoff exceeding its storage capacity is safely directed away rather than backing up and flooding nearby plantings, pavement, or structures. Sealing the basin with an impermeable liner would defeat the purpose of infiltration, grading the area to exclude the downspout water largely contradicts the design intent, and connecting a stormwater feature to a drinking water supply is inappropriate and not a grading concern.
  129. The correct answers are A and D: the average end area method takes the average of two adjacent cross-sectional areas and multiplies that average by the distance between the stations to find volume, and since 1 cubic yard equals 27 cubic feet, the resulting cubic foot volume must be divided by 27 to express the result in cubic yards. Statement B is false because station spacing can vary based on how the terrain changes, and statement C is false because the same method applies equally to cut and fill cross sections.
  130. The correct answer is C (Following existing natural contours as closely as possible rather than a straight cut-and-fill alignment): aligning a trail with the natural topography minimizes the amount of cut and fill needed, which in turn reduces disturbance to tree roots and existing grade near mature trees. A rigidly straight alignment, a uniform deep cut everywhere, and routing along the steepest slopes would all tend to increase disturbance and earthwork rather than minimize it.
  131. The correct answer is A (Water crossing the sidewalk could create a cross slope or ponding that violates accessible route requirements): allowing a swale to cross an accessible sidewalk at grade risks creating an excessive cross slope dip or standing water that would violate accessible route slope and surface requirements, so a culvert or pipe beneath the walk is typically needed instead. This condition does not automatically affect the running slope, does not by itself require doubling the sidewalk width, and does raise a genuine accessibility concern rather than a purely aesthetic one.
  132. The correct answer is A (The inlet rim should sit at or below the surrounding finish grade so water flows in): for an inlet to effectively capture surface runoff, its rim elevation must be at or slightly lower than the surrounding finish grade so water is drawn toward and into the structure rather than diverted around it. Setting the rim above finish grade, treating it as unrelated to finish grade, or matching it to the plaza's highest point would each interfere with the inlet's ability to collect surface flow.
  133. The correct answer is C (Calculate the estimated post-development runoff and compare it against the swale's conveyance capacity): evaluating whether the existing swale can handle increased flow requires calculating the anticipated post-development runoff and comparing it to the swale's actual hydraulic capacity, rather than assuming past performance under different, lower-flow conditions will continue to hold. Filling the swale without analysis, assuming adequacy without checking, and increasing upstream paving to simplify the math are not legitimate engineering approaches.
  134. The correct answer is B (Spot elevations at each grade break let the contractor build accurately and verify slope compliance): providing precise spot elevations at ramps, steps, walls, and other grade breaks gives the contractor exact construction targets and allows the design team to confirm that resulting slopes meet applicable accessibility and drainage requirements. This level of detail serves a functional construction and compliance purpose, is not merely aesthetic, does not replace contour lines used elsewhere on the plan, and applies regardless of whether water features are present.
  135. The correct answer is D (Detailed, or fine, grading design of the paved surface area): establishing the specific high points, low points, and slope directions across a paved area to direct stormwater toward inlets is the work of detailed, or fine, grading design, which is distinct from structural pavement section design, traffic striping layout, and irrigation zoning, each of which addresses a different aspect of the site.
  136. The correct answers are A and B: percent slope is defined as rise divided by run, multiplied by 100, and a ratio slope of 1:20 means 1 unit of rise for every 20 units of run, which works out to 1 divided by 20 times 100, or 5 percent. Statement C reverses the correct formula, dividing run by rise instead, and statement D is backwards, since a steeper slope always corresponds to a larger, not smaller, percent slope value.
  137. The correct answer is B (Yes, a 1:15 slope is less steep than 1:12, since a larger denominator means flatter): In a slope ratio written as 1 to a number, a larger second number means more horizontal run per unit of rise, which is a flatter slope, so 1:15 is less steep than 1:12 and complies with the maximum ramp slope requirement. Option D is incorrect because 1:15 and 1:12 are different slope values, not very similar ones, and option A describes a restriction that does not exist.
  138. The correct answer is D (The finish grade elevation difference between each terrace's top and bottom at the wall): a retaining wall's required height is determined directly by the elevation difference between the finish grade on the high side and the finish grade on the low side at that specific wall location. The planting area's square footage, the plant species selected, and the terrace's compass orientation do not determine the structural height needed for the wall.
  139. The correct answer is A (A French drain, since it is designed to intercept subsurface groundwater rather than surface flow): since the problem is described as groundwater seeping to the surface rather than surface runoff, a French drain, with its perforated pipe set in gravel below grade, is generally the more appropriate tool for intercepting that subsurface flow before it emerges. A swale primarily manages surface flow and does not directly lower a water table, and there is no reason a subsurface drain could not be installed at the base of a slope.
  140. The correct answer is C (The property line grade should match existing grade to avoid altering drainage onto the neighboring lot): where a neighboring property cannot be disturbed, the proposed grading must tie in smoothly with existing grade at the shared property line so that drainage patterns onto the adjacent lot are not changed. Arbitrarily raising or lowering the grade at that line, or assuming it has no bearing on neighboring drainage, both risk creating a drainage conflict with the adjacent property owner.
  141. The correct answer is D (It lets a reviewer compare existing and proposed conditions to understand cut and fill extent): overlaying existing dashed contours with proposed solid contours lets a reviewer visually see where the ground will be raised or lowered and by roughly how much, which supports understanding the overall grading concept. This convention does not replace the need for calculated earthwork volumes, is not simply a software limitation, and provides a genuine technical benefit rather than being purely stylistic.
  142. The correct answer is B (To direct surface water away from the foundation, reducing infiltration and basement moisture risk): grading finish surfaces to slope away from a building for the first several feet is a standard practice to keep surface water moving away from the foundation, reducing the risk of infiltration, moisture damage, or basement flooding. This requirement is functional rather than purely decorative, is unrelated to total earthwork quantity, and does not by itself guarantee compliance with the accessible route running slope standard elsewhere on the plaza.
  143. Statement A is false because vertical curves occur at both high points, called crest curves, and low points, called sag curves, and statement B is false because vertical curve data addresses only the roadway's vertical profile, not its separate horizontal alignment.
  144. The correct answer is C (Tracing the contour lines to identify closed loops that indicate a depression rather than a hill): a closed contour loop can represent either a hill or a depression, so tracing the sequence of contour values inward, whether they increase toward a high point or decrease toward a low point, reveals whether an area is a potential ponding depression. Counting trees, measuring hydrant distance, and reviewing a fencing specification are unrelated to identifying low spots in the proposed grading.
  145. The correct answer is A (An open swale can add ecological, aesthetic, and water quality benefits where space allows): where space allows, an open vegetated swale can offer benefits beyond simple conveyance, including habitat value, visual interest, and some water quality treatment through vegetation and infiltration, which an enclosed pipe cannot provide. A swale's hydraulic capacity depends on its specific dimensions and slope rather than being universally larger than a pipe, a swale still needs an adequate minimum slope, and erosion control is still required during its construction.
  146. The correct answer is A (Flag the discrepancy and confirm the correct flow direction with the design team first): whenever a plan drawing and a written narrative disagree on a fundamental fact like flow direction, the reviewer's responsibility is to identify the conflict and resolve it with the design team rather than guessing which source is correct. Assuming either the drawing or the narrative is automatically correct without verification, or ignoring the conflict altogether, all risk allowing a real design error to go uncorrected.
  147. A perfectly flat tread, a slope directing water into the riser, and a material choice based only on color would each fail to address proper drainage of the tiered seating.
  148. The correct answer is B (A catch basin has a grate to collect stormwater, while a manhole mainly provides pipe access): a catch basin is built with a grate or curb inlet specifically to capture surface runoff, while a manhole is typically a closed, flush-lidded structure used mainly for maintenance access to a pipe network rather than for direct surface water collection. The two are not interchangeable names, manholes are also used in storm drain systems for access, not only sanitary systems, and relative size is not a defining functional distinction between the two.
  149. The correct answer is D (A drainage layer, such as a lightweight mat or panel, that channels water toward roof drains): green roof assemblies typically include a dedicated drainage layer directly above the waterproofing membrane that allows excess water to move laterally toward roof drains while retaining some moisture for the plants above. A French drain and a culvert are ground-level or subsurface components not typically used within a roof assembly, and a perimeter trench drain is not the standard component used for this specific membrane-level drainage function.
  150. The correct answers are B, C, and D: a mismatch between the grading plan and the roadway profile at the same location is a real inconsistency, an upstream invert set lower than the downstream invert would mean the pipe flows uphill, which is a real design error, and a catch basin appearing on the drainage plan but missing from the grading plan is a genuine coordination gap. Statement A describes the correct, desired condition, since proposed contours should agree with the architectural finish floor elevations, so it is not itself a red flag.
  151. The correct answer is B (Top of curb and flow line, meaning the curb's upper edge and the gutter's low elevation): TC and FL are standard grading plan abbreviations for top of curb and flow line, and showing both at the same location lets a reviewer confirm the curb height and the gutter's drainage elevation are correctly related to each other. The other interpretations describing budget, planting, or construction phasing information do not match how these abbreviations are conventionally used on grading plans.
  152. The correct answer is D (The basin's excavated depth and plan area below the elevation of its outlet control structure): a detention basin's usable storage volume is determined by how much excavated depth and plan area exist below the elevation at which the outlet control structure begins releasing water, since that combination defines how much runoff can be temporarily held. The turf species, riprap appearance, and distance to a roadway do not determine the basin's actual storage capacity.
  153. The correct answer is A (That the underlying soil or aggregate base can adequately infiltrate the water passing through the pavers): permeable pavers only function as an effective stormwater management tool if the subgrade soil or aggregate reservoir beneath them can actually accept and store or infiltrate the water passing through, so confirming that infiltration capacity is essential before finalizing the design. Paver pattern, color matching, and green roof ballast requirements are unrelated to whether the system will function hydrologically as intended.
  154. The correct answer is C (Tie new drainage into the swale at a rate that does not alter its function): respecting a conservation easement over a natural swale means designing new drainage to connect to it without altering its natural character, flow pattern, or capacity, rather than eliminating or relocating the protected feature. Filling in the swale, ignoring the easement, or relocating the natural feature would all violate the intent of a conservation easement protecting that drainage corridor.
  155. The correct answer is D (Provide a secondary overflow path, such as a curb opening, in case the inlet is blocked): Good drainage design typically provides a secondary overflow route so that if the primary inlet becomes blocked by debris, runoff still has a safe path rather than uncontrolled ponding or flooding. Removing the catch basin largely, increasing the paved area, and relocating the inlet away from the actual low point would not solve the underlying redundancy problem being identified.
  156. The correct answer is B (The accessible route's cross slope through the driveway apron should still comply with the maximum limit): where an accessible route crosses a driveway, the transition through the apron must still be designed to keep the route's cross slope within the standard maximum, since accessibility requirements do not relax simply because a vehicular crossing is present. The apron's grading directly affects whether the crossing sidewalk segment remains compliant, so it cannot be treated as unrelated or exempt from the standard.
  157. The correct answer is C (To preserve a clear, unobstructed path for stormwater to keep flowing across the property): a drainage easement legally protects a defined corridor for stormwater conveyance across a property, so grading and structures must respect that corridor to avoid obstructing established flow paths and violating the recorded easement. Drainage easements do not typically expire after a fixed term to become buildable area, are not primarily aesthetic buffers, and are not designated specifically for overhead utility installation.
  158. The correct answer is A (Grade the paving around the fountain to slope gently away from the basin toward a drain): sloping the surrounding paving away from the fountain basin toward a nearby drain ensures that splash and overflow water is carried away rather than pooling around the fountain. Sloping the paving toward the basin, setting the basin lower than the drain inlet, and omitting a nearby drain entirely would each increase the risk of standing water around the fountain.
  159. The correct answer is A (A hydraulic analysis comparing the design storm flow rate to the culvert's capacity and headwater elevation): selecting an appropriately sized culvert requires a hydraulic analysis that checks whether the culvert can convey the design storm flow without raising the upstream headwater elevation beyond an acceptable limit. A visual color match, a cost-only comparison ignoring hydraulics, and a purely aesthetic resident survey do not address whether the culvert will actually function correctly during a storm event.
  160. The correct answer is C (A 6 percent slope exceeds the 5 percent maximum running slope for an accessible route): since the maximum running slope for an accessible route, outside a properly designed ramp with handrails and landings, is 5 percent, a calculated 6 percent slope on this play surface would not comply and should be flagged for regrading. This slope concern applies regardless of the specific play surfacing material, and a 6 percent slope does not automatically satisfy the separate design requirements for a compliant ramp.
  161. The correct answer is B (The landing must remain essentially level, with a slope no greater than about 2 percent): even when a ramp landing also functions as a viewing terrace, it must remain essentially level, typically limited to about a 2 percent maximum slope, to allow safe standing, resting, and maneuvering between ramp runs. Matching the steeper ramp slope, sloping continuously with the hillside, or having no slope limit at all would each undermine the landing's required function as a level resting point.
  162. The correct answer is D (Drainage arrows showing the intended direction of surface flow at key locations across the site): drainage arrows are a standard, efficient way to communicate the intended direction of surface runoff at critical locations, helping a contractor unfamiliar with the site quickly understand how the grading is meant to function. A plant list, a furniture layout, and a lighting plan each convey different design information and do not communicate drainage direction.
  163. The correct answer is B (All drainage must be directed to roof drains or scuppers, since no ground infiltration exists): unlike ground-level grading, a rooftop terrace sits above a waterproofed structural slab with no soil beneath it to infiltrate water, so all surface drainage must be reliably directed to roof drains or scuppers to prevent ponding and potential leaks. Assuming natural infiltration is available, treating grading tolerances as less critical due to smaller area, and assuming no slope is needed all misunderstand the unique drainage demands of a rooftop condition.
  164. The correct answer is D (Calculate the actual percent slope between adjacent spot elevations and compare it to the noted value): the only reliable way to confirm internal consistency is to independently calculate the percent slope from the actual spot elevations and distances shown, then compare that calculated value to whatever slope is noted on the plan. Simply assuming the noted value is correct, comparing elevations to an unrelated plant list, or comparing the slope note to the drawing scale bar would not verify whether the numbers are actually consistent.
  165. The correct answer is A (A catch basin sized to accept both driveway sheet flow and adjacent landscape runoff): a catch basin placed at the converging low point can be sized to accept combined flow from both the driveway surface and adjacent landscape areas, making it well suited to this type of combined drainage condition. A French drain is a subsurface groundwater structure not intended for surface sheet flow, a culvert is meant to convey flow beneath a crossing rather than collect converging surface runoff, and omitting a structure entirely would leave the low point without any means of managing the converging flow.
  166. The correct answer is C (To confirm spot elevations, slopes, and drainage structure locations are consistent across every sheet): a final coordination check exists to catch discrepancies between related sheets, such as a spot elevation, slope value, or drainage structure location shown differently on the grading plan than on the drainage plan or profile sheets, before the set is issued for construction. Matching title block formatting, plant list quantities, and construction budget figures are important in their own right but are not the specific purpose of a grading and drainage coordination check.
